0,2026-10-13,"Four months ago it was the market's biggest drag, and shareholders are still suing over the fall
Microsoft is on pace for its first record close since October 2025, having gained more than 50% from a June low and added roughly $1.5T in market value. The AI-disruption panic that caused the fall also hit SAP, which has not recovered.
Microsoft is on track for its first record close in almost a year, though it has not got there yet. The stock last set a closing high in October 2025.
Shares rose as much as 1.8% to $551.13 on Tuesday morning in New York, Bloomberg reported. Monday’s close of $541.15 had left it just short of its October 2025 peak, at a market value of $4T.
The swing has been violent in both directions. Microsoft has gained more than 50% from a June low, adding roughly $1.5T in market value.
June was the worst of it. The stock fell 17% that month, its worst since 2000, erasing nearly $600B as investors doubted AI infrastructure spending was earning anything back.
By late July it was down 18.7% for the year, and the options market was pricing a $190B swing on one earnings report. Sentiment had moved from faith to accounting.
The report answered the question. Azure grew 43% against the 40% expected, the fastest cloud growth in four years and well clear of the guidance that triggered the sell-off.
It also pushed Azure past $100B in annual revenue for the first time. Shares jumped 16% the next day, and the company went on to its best quarter since 1998.
Analysts followed the price. Of 72 tracked by Bloomberg, 68 rate the stock a buy and none a sell, with an average target of $584 implying about 8% more.
Not everyone is celebrating. Shareholders sued Microsoft in June over the collapse that preceded all of this, and the case has not been resolved.
A Michigan police and fire pension fund alleges the company concealed that Azure growth was slowing and that heavy AI spending would be needed. The claim covers May 2025 to January 2026.
Europe got the same fear and not the same cure. The AI-disruption panic that hit Microsoft also hit SAP’s shares, down about 35% for the year by late July.
They have not come back. SAP traded at €193.91 on Monday, around 20.6% below its 52-week high, worth about €224B against the €313B that made it Europe’s most valuable company in March 2025.
The mood is not uniform. South Korea’s Kospi fell more than 7% intraday on Tuesday, with Samsung down 9%, and Broadcom’s chief executive said many AI investors have no clue what they are funding.

1,2026-10-13,"Apple's share of worldwide smartphone shipments rose to a third-quarter record of 22% in the third quarter of 2026, despite a decline in its own iPhone shipments, according to preliminary figures from Counterpoint Research.
Apple finished the quarter just behind Samsung, which accounted for 23% of shipments. Across the industry, manufacturers shipped 20% fewer smartphones than a year earlier, taking the July-to-September total to its lowest level since 2012.
Apple's launch plan was a significant factor in its lower shipment volume this year. With no standard iPhone 18 arriving alongside the iPhone 18 Pro models in September, Counterpoint said Apple supplied fewer new devices to retail channels than during last year's iPhone 17 lineup launch.
The research firm nevertheless reported a better debut for the ‌iPhone 18 Pro‌ models than for the previous Pro generation, with sales in China and India standing out. Demand for the ‌iPhone 17‌ also continued to support shipments.
Apple also outperformed the wider market in China. Separate IDC figures reported by Reuters put Apple's shipment decline at 4.3%, compared with a 15.4% contraction for the country as a whole. China's total of 57.9 million units marked its sixth consecutive quarter of annual declines.
Apple took second place in China with a 17.8% share, behind Huawei at 21%. IDC said Apple's newly released iPhones exceeded sales expectations, although the smaller selection of new models reduced its overall shipments.
Counterpoint linked the global downturn to shortages and rising prices for memory chips, which have made phones more expensive to produce. It estimates that smartphone retail prices have risen 20% globally so far this year, with demand for premium smartphones proving to be more resilient than budget devices.

2,2026-10-13,"Hyperscalers now issue 10% of the world’s investment-grade bonds, up from under 1% in 2024. The IMF also says software makers had almost no time to patch flaws in 70% of cases this year.
The International Monetary Fund’s headquarters in Washington
A correction in AI-related stocks and debt could set off market turmoil, the International Monetary Fund said on Tuesday. It named the AI risk as one of three that could sharply tighten financial conditions. The other two are a sharp rise in government bond yields and cyber threats.
The warning came in the IMF’s Global Financial Stability Report at the annual meetings in Bangkok. Weaker or less durable AI investment and profits could trigger the correction, the Fund said. Rising borrowing, concentrated exposures and circular AI deals could make it worse.
The IMF reviewed 14 publicly disclosed deals since 2023. In them, investors led by hyperscalers put about $300bn of equity into financially weaker AI firms, alongside $650bn in debt. In return, they secured $1.4tn in future revenue commitments through services or chip orders.
About $320bn of that debt sits with the firms receiving the money, the report said. A handful of them hold most of the financing and revenue promises.
The IMF called it “wrong-way risk”: “Equity and debt investments in the firms depreciate in value precisely when future revenue streams tied to them are impaired.”
IMF assistant director Jason Wu called it a “triple whammy” at a press briefing, CNBC reported.
Hyperscalers, the biggest data centre operators, now issue 10% of the world’s investment-grade bonds. In 2024, it was less than 1%. Their free cash flow is falling fast as they keep spending on AI, the IMF said. That makes debt increasingly necessary.
Other AI firms, such as neocloud operators, carry more debt and are less profitable. The report said this raises the possibility of weak links. A law firm recently mapped how AI data centre debt could unravel.
Investors are borrowing too. Hedge funds’ gross equity positions plus margin loans have grown since 2024. They now equal about 15% of advanced economies’ GDP, up from 10%. Retail investors are using geared exchange-traded funds, which magnify returns, to bet on AI stocks. Single stocks now make up 28% of these funds, up from 16%.
Advanced Asian stock markets, which move most closely with global AI shares, would likely suffer the most in a correction. The euro area and the UK are less sensitive. Stress could still reach them through their sharply higher holdings of US shares.
On cyber threats, the IMF said state-sponsored groups, ransomware gangs and insiders now have access to frontier AI models. Those models have shown strong capabilities in network-attack simulations. In June, IMF chief Kristalina Georgieva warned that Mythos-class AI models could be turned against the financial system.
Attackers are also moving faster. In more than 70% of cases this year, software providers had almost no time to patch a flaw before it was exploited. That is 20 percentage points more than a year ago. In 2021, providers typically had close to 300 days, the IMF said in a blog post.
The IMF’s 2026 bank stress test combined an energy price shock, higher inflation and bond yields, and an AI-led market correction. Banks would remain broadly resilient, the Fund said. But banks holding 22% of global banking assets would end up weak, with core capital ratios below 7%. A year ago, that share was 18%.
The Fund called on authorities to contain excessive borrowing behind AI bets, including limits on margin loans, geared exchange-traded products and derivatives. It also urged governments to put public debt on a sustainable path and strengthen cyber defences.
In its World Economic Outlook, also published on Tuesday, the IMF raised its 2026 global growth forecast to 3.1%, from 3% in July. On Monday, Georgieva said AI investment is keeping global growth near 3%.

3,2026-10-13,"If you’ve been reading Hackaday for any length of time, you’ll know we love seeing hackers replacing the stock firmware on their devices with open source alternatives. Usually this is done because the open firmware offers more features, or lifts some arbitrary limitation. But if you find yourself installing OSIRIS on an Onkyo AV receiver such as the TX-NR717, it’s probably because you’re trying to bring the thing back to life.
As developer [Jokūbas] explains, certain Onkyo receivers that use the Texas Instruments D830 are known to suffer from a very specific failure — the device will power on and appear functional, but no audio will come through. When this happens, it’s either because the D830 itself has failed or the 64 MiB NAND flash chip that holds the firmware it runs is cooked. Unfortunately, replacing either component is a bit more difficult than you might expect.
If you swap the D830 with a pin-compatible chip that’s easier to find and less prone to failure, the stock firmware won’t boot. If you try to replace the NAND, you’ll find that the original software is only capable of addressing 512-byte pages, while the flash you’ll find on the market today uses 2 KiB or 4 KiB pages. In both cases, it’s OSIRIS to the rescue.
The open firmware not only supports D830 compatible chips such as the OMAP-L137, but it’s able to address modern NAND flash, making the replacement of either or both component far more practical. With fresh hardware and the new firmware installed, your Onkyo receiver should spring back to life. That said, OSIRIS isn’t feature complete yet, and truthfully the items listed as untested or not implemented on the feature list currently outnumber the entries for what actually does work. But at least it’s a start.
Of course, not all the burned out parts on your Onkyo receiver require a firmware flash to replace. Sometimes a misbehaving unit just needs to have a few of its passive components replaced.
D830? Isn’t it a DA830?
So you got a doc and you’re still complaining ?

The global phone market looked bleaker and bleaker after every quarter in 2026. And new research states this is the worst it's been in 14 years.
Counterpoint Research analysts have published their Q3 2026 findings, which show just how far global phone shipments have plummeted. According to the research, global shipments saw a 20% YoY decline — and the culprit is clear. ""The extreme memory shortage is pushing the smartphone market into a more selective phase,"" said Counterpoint senior analyst Shilpi Jain.
It states this is the lowest global phone shipments have been since 2012. While a sharp decline, Counterpoint states Samsung has retained its top position in the past quarter with a 3% YoY decline. What's more, Samsung's market share reportedly increased by 23%, ""the largest share gain among major OEMs."" Researchers attribute this to the Galaxy Z Fold 8 and Fold 8 Ultra sales and its Galaxy A series.
The extreme memory shortage is pushing the smartphone market into a more selective phase
Apple sits right behind Samsung in Q3, and the iPhone lineup is informative: The iPhone 18 series lacks a base option, offering only an 18 Pro and an 18 Pro Max. Counterpoint's analytics suggest this warranted the decline. Additionally, Chinese OEMs (Xiaomi, OPPO, and Vivo) round out the top five with massive declines of their own.
While a shortage of DRAM and NAND (courtesy of AI) is a key player in the market's decline, Counterpoint states ""consumer behavior"" has also changed as a result. Jain said, ""Consumers are holding on to their devices longer than estimated, increasingly considering refurbished devices and prioritizing higher-value devices.""
Q4 2026 remains dire for global phone shipments, with the post stating a recovery isn't likely ""until component costs and supply conditions improve.""
 



The idea of consumers hanging onto their phones longer is an interesting note for me. You end up having to make a decision: Do I buy this new phone I want that costs over $1,000, or do I keep the phone I already have? I hear every day how people are struggling with money. At the end of the day, you need a device that does what you need it to. If that's a phone that you bought a year or two ago, why get a new one when that money is better spent elsewhere?
Sentiments surrounding Q3 and Q4 of 2026 have not changed. In July, Omdia posted its research on the global phone market for Q2. The market dipped by 4% YoY in Q2; however, Samsung and Apple saw growth of 2% and 4%, respectively, compared with other OEMs. While Omdia highlighted that the memory crisis caused the Q2 decline, it also forecasted continued turbulence for Q3 and Q4.

5,2026-10-13,"Your browser does not support the video element.
Your browser does not support the video element.
As they race to automate white-collar work, AI labs have found the perfect source of training data: your Slack DMs.
Business owners are getting six-figure ""exploding offers"" that expire in 48 hours to sell their data. Instagram is flooded with ads declaring that ""AI needs your data"" and promising millions. The data is priced by the terabyte, anonymized, and sold to AI giants desperate for an edge.
Tech giants have moved to harvest their employees' data for AI training. And, once focused on dead startups, brokers now pay for live data from active companies.
""Right now, the demand is just astronomical,"" said Dan Novaes, the CEO of Mode.Inc, a startup that sells data to AI model developers.
Mundane workplace data is more valuable than ever as OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta, and others build AI agents for businesses. Annual spending on data for AI is projected to explode from less than $1 billion last year to almost $6.5 billion in 2027, Gartner estimates.
The boom has a darker side. Employees rarely profit from — or know about — their Slacks, PowerPoints, and Google Docs being sold to the highest bidder. Suspected hackers have shopped around a large company's stolen codebase for millions of dollars. Meta's attempt to harvest its own employees' data for its AI models sparked a revolt.
This story is part of a series called The AI Data Grab, an inside look at how tech giants are increasingly turning to workers, users, and the physical world to get data to train AI. Read more stories in this series here.
Some CEOs are getting blindsided. Doug Kreuzkamp, who runs airline operations company Springshot, was sitting in his office on a Zoom call in August when he found out that Google had bought his data. Springshot, a longtime vendor for the now-bankrupt Spirit Airlines, helped flights coordinate with workers such as gate agents and cleaners.
""I had less than 48 hours' notice — from the media — that the intellectual property we had spent 15 years building could be given away,"" Kreuzkamp told Business Insider.
The surging market is pushing into uncharted waters, and the industry risks a reckoning with workers and regulators, said Ifeoma Ajunwa, a law professor at Emory University. The lack of federal privacy laws leaves few legal barriers to companies collecting data from their employees for AI training, she added.
""The question is, does the consent of the employees from whom they collected that data actually extend to the sale of the data? I think it's an ethically gray area,"" Ajunwa said.
The boom has created a thriving group of go-betweens who hunt for small businesses rich in data and low on cash. After scrubbing sensitive information like Social Security numbers and home addresses, they resell it to AI giants.
Although the industry is new, it's become highly competitive. Ryan Lock, cofounder of AI broker Polyshares, compares it to the oil boom that hit Texas in the early 1900s, when prospectors snapped up cheap land from farmers who didn't realize the true value of the oil underneath it.
Small-business owners sitting on troves of internal records may not realize the seemingly mundane files could be worth big money. Tactics like 48-hour deadlines have become common, Lock said.
""The offers being made look almost identical to those early oil offers,"" he said from his home base in Dallas. ""They'll say, 'I'm offering you double market. I need an answer by Friday.'""
Lock and his cofounders went through the data-licensing process themselves with their other companies, sometimes laughing out loud at the lowball offers they got. Their firm tries to bridge that information gap by giving business owners more transparency about what their data is worth.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
""There's still plenty of money for everyone, but business owners should receive more of the benefit,"" he said.
Data that demonstrates how work is actually done is highly valuable, said William Almeida, the chief revenue officer of AI training startup Micro1, because it provides critical context on how decisions are made and products are built.
That information can help train agents, the autonomous computer-using AI tools that have become a major focus for companies like Meta and SpaceXAI. As AI companies build new models, the real-world data guides the AI on how best to handle specific tasks.
Micro1 announced a program earlier this year that pays midsize companies $100,000 to $2 million for access to anonymized data. Almeida said AI developers increasingly want ""long-horizon"" data that can provide a full picture of how work gets done. If a company won't sell its Slack, customer relationship management, or email records, the rest of the corpus is less valuable.
The data is priced based on a multitude of factors, with volume being a critical one. The more white-collar employees a company has, the more valuable its data corpus is, said Novaes, the Mode.Inc CEO.
Other factors, like exclusivity, matter — there's lower demand for a codebase that a rival lab already has its hands on. Fields that AI giants are trying the hardest to automate, like law and accounting, see especially high demand.
Generally, though, any company — dead or alive, big or small — can sell its data, as long as it has enough.
""People need to wake up to it and take this opportunity,"" Novaes said.
Grant Gooley decided to license his company's data to an AI training company. His agency, MyPortal Marketing, has sold Micro1 everything from Figma and Canva files to checklists for onboarding clients to Google ads. He said it didn't sell client-specific material.
""If AI is going to be trained, why not be part of that, and do it responsibly?"" Gooley said.
While the market creates wealth for some, it's breeding new kinds of cybersecurity and privacy risks for others.
Nick Heiner helps improve AI agents by building office-like digital environments at AI training startup Surge AI. Heiner said that earlier this year, he was approached by a group offering a large company's codebase for millions of dollars.
""It became apparent that this group did not have the rights to sell this data,"" Heiner told Business Insider. ""They had illicitly obtained a copy of the codebase and were just going around to all the data vendors saying, 'Can I get a few million bucks for this?'""
Security and consent concerns aren't limited to hackers. Google's bid to buy Spirit Airlines' old data for $10 million has been held up, in part, by flight attendants worried about how their records will be used. Last week, Sen. Elizabeth Warren and over 100 other members of Congress wrote a letter to Google and Spirit urging the companies to protect the privacy of thousands of former Spirit employees.
A Google spokesperson told Business Insider that it's not looking to buy any personal information from Spirit Airlines and that any such data would be either excluded or de-identified by an independent third party. A representative for Spirit said the company is working closely with the flight attendants and other unions to address their concerns.
Former gig workers for Mercor, another AI training startup, have alleged in an ongoing lawsuit that the company encouraged them to upload work materials without their employers' consent. Mercor has denied their claims in court.
The hunger for work records is so strong that some tech giants have tried tracking their own staff.
At Meta, employees revolted when the company announced a new program earlier this year to track their keystrokes and mouse movements for AI training. Employees worried that Meta would capture their sensitive data, like credit card numbers and immigration documents.
Although Meta's chief technology officer, Andrew Bosworth, pledged that the data would be ""tightly controlled,"" it turned out that it had been viewable across the company in a major internal leak. The supposedly anonymized data included employees' private conversations and performance records, screenshots obtained by Business Insider show. Meta suspended the project shortly after the breach and hasn't restarted it.
Google once considered tracking its staff's AI output and ultimately dropped the idea, Business Insider previously reported.
The Meta revolt hasn't stopped other companies from eyeing employee data as a useful shortcut to AI domination, including the world's richest man.
In an all-hands meeting in August, CEO Elon Musk told employees that SpaceX would train its Grok AI on worker data and encouraged them to think of themselves as the model's ""parents.""
""In a way, it will be trained on you. It will inherit your thoughts and ideas and beliefs, and I think that's a good thing,"" Musk said.
Though the company data market is nascent, the AI training startups cashing in are optimistic.
Ali Ansari, Micro1's CEO, says licensing data to labs could become one of the most common revenue sources for businesses worldwide, he recently posted on X. He estimates that, soon, about 10% of the US's GDP could be dedicated to training AI models, thanks to business data and human professionals.
Last week, Micro1 announced a commitment to spend $1 billion on company data over the next 12 months.
The growing use of employee records for AI training could heighten the urgency of calls for new data protection and privacy regulations, said Alex Rosenblat, a sociologist and former Uber policy executive.
It could also give employees new leverage and negotiating power as the value of their everyday digital footprint grows, she said.
""It's not that data collection wasn't present before; it's that the scale at which it can happen, the scale at which it is useful, has shifted,"" Rosenblat said.
""And that's an opportunity for people to say, 'wait a minute, the value of this is escaping us.'""

7,2026-10-13,"Apple's defending its distribution moat by setting up one around your home.
At 9 a.m. ET, the company will hold an event — ""Welcome Home"" — where it's expected to unveil some smart-home devices as part of its bigger strategy in the space.
Details are scant. Apple's teaser only includes a catchy tune and someone doing a trendy hobby (cross-stitching).
But according to Bloomberg's Mark Gurman, we'll learn more about HomeView, which will serve as a sort of hub to interact with and direct Apple's smart devices in your home.
It's a big push for Apple into the smart-home market, where it hasn't succeeded as much as rivals Amazon and Google. But the decision feels more substantial than just taking on Amazon's Alexa and Google Home.It's been a remarkable few months for consumer hardware.
In September, Meta surprised everyone with an AI device called Charm tied to its buzzy AI agent, Muse. It also showed off its new VR Glasses, which are lighter and cheaper than the Apple Vision Pro.
Charm is scheduled to ship for the holidays, while the Meta VR goggles are coming next spring.
The following week, OpenAI announced its own AI agent, Dots. While there was no major hardware announcement, the long-term goal is to release something with iPhone designer Jony Ive. OpenAI CFO Sarah Friar teased it a bit this June.
All of this matters because these hardware products mean tech companies would no longer rely on Apple for distribution. The iPhone's massive reach is essentially a toll tech companies must pay to get their apps into people's hands.

That's allowed Apple to avoid investing as heavily in AI because it controls the key path to consumers. It also hasn't forced it to rethink the iPhone too much. After all, why upset the golden goose?
Investors have rewarded Apple for its avoidance of massive AI budgets. Its stock is up more than 25% this year, outperforming Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, and Microsoft.
But the past few months show the potential for change.
Look how quickly Apple's big hardware announcement last month, the iPhone Duo, has taken a back seat to chatter about AI agents and their accompanying devices. Meta's stock is up more than 11% over the past month, while Apple's is up just over 2%.
That's why pushing into smart-home hardware makes sense. If Apple can cement its hardware in your home while it still has you hooked on the iPhone, that represents another hurdle for tech rivals.
At a time when AI is making us rethink our interactions with technology, Apple wants to make sure its house is in order. And the best way to do that is to go after yours.

8,2026-10-13,"DeepSigma has launched a paper-trading demo covering 77 traditional financial markets, using patented blockchain technology to bring crypto-style perpetual futures to stocks, commodities and other real-world assets.
A team with backgrounds at Goldman Sachs, Citadel Securities, Google and Uber is developing a decentralized exchange designed to bring one of crypto’s most popular trading models into traditional financial markets.
The platform, called DeepSigma, has unveiled a live paper-trading demo supporting 77 real-world asset (RWA) perpetual contracts, spanning U.S. stocks, major indexes, commodities, sector ETFs and Treasury yields.
Powered by the Cypherium blockchain, DeepSigma aims to let traders take long or short positions on traditional assets without owning, tokenising or taking custody of the underlying instruments. Instead, its perpetual contracts track real-world markets using external price data.
The project brings together experience from both traditional finance and blockchain development. Its leadership includes Albert Xu, a former Goldman Sachs FICC portfolio manager; Jenny Chen, a quantitative macro trader who most recently worked at Citadel Securities; and Chaoran Wang, a former Google and Uber engineer who founded quantitative trading firm UmiUmi.
Leading the company is CEO Sky Guo, who also founded Cypherium, the Layer 1 blockchain powering the exchange.
Perpetual futures have become a major part of cryptocurrency trading, allowing traders to maintain leveraged market exposure without the fixed expiration dates associated with traditional futures contracts.
But applying the same model to traditional financial assets introduces a different set of technical challenges.
Unlike cryptocurrencies, which trade continuously, stocks and many other traditional assets operate within defined market hours. Their underlying markets can close overnight or over weekends, creating complications for pricing, liquidity and risk management on a platform designed for continuous trading.
“Crypto has already demonstrated the demand for perpetual markets, but you cannot simply take the same model and put a stock or commodity ticker on it,” said Guo. “Real-world markets have different trading hours, liquidity conditions, pricing sources and regulatory considerations. We started DeepSigma with those differences in mind.”
DeepSigma’s demo currently covers markets including the S&P 500, Dow Jones Industrial Average, Nasdaq Composite, Russell 2000 and VIX, alongside commodities such as gold, silver, crude oil, natural gas and copper.
To address the differences between crypto and traditional markets, the company is developing a risk framework that includes multiple external pricing sources, position and exposure limits, circuit breakers, partial liquidations and an insurance mechanism for stressed market conditions.
The exchange uses a central limit order book model, supported by a clearing layer responsible for managing positions, collateral, margin and settlement.
“The hardest part of bringing real-world assets on-chain isn’t creating another market symbol. It’s making sure the market still behaves sensibly when the underlying asset is closed, liquidity disappears or an external price moves sharply,” Guo said.
Regulatory compliance is another area where DeepSigma is attempting to differentiate itself from fully permissionless crypto trading platforms.
The company says it has designed its approach around U.S. regulatory frameworks from the outset and is working with specialized digital-asset legal counsel.
Part of that strategy involves developing decentralized identity infrastructure using zero-knowledge proofs.
Under the proposed system, participants would be able to demonstrate that they meet certain eligibility requirements without exposing the personal information behind those credentials on-chain.
DeepSigma describes the principle as “prove eligibility, not identity,” an approach intended to combine regulatory requirements with the privacy associated with decentralized financial systems.
The company has not presented this identity infrastructure as a completed feature of its current paper-trading demo.
DeepSigma runs on Cypherium, an open, permissionless Layer 1 blockchain founded by Guo.
The underlying blockchain technology is covered by four granted U.S. patents relating to consensus and associated blockchain systems.
Cypherium combines Proof-of-Work with a HotStuff-derived Byzantine Fault Tolerant consensus mechanism. The blockchain is designed to handle hundreds of thousands of transactions per second, with the goal of supporting high-throughput financial applications.
Guo is a named inventor on Cypherium’s patented technology. He also previously co-authored DLT Options for CBDC with Thomas Moser of the Swiss National Bank and has spoken at the United Nations Headquarters and Nasdaq.
Beyond its blockchain infrastructure, DeepSigma’s current trading terminal includes market data, technical indicators, asset-specific news, portfolio tracking and a competitive leaderboard.
It also features an AI Market Analysis Desk that can analyze markets and generate potential trading strategies. The AI component operates entirely off-chain and has no authority to execute transactions or move user assets.
For now, the platform’s publicly described trading functionality remains a paper-trading demo rather than a live exchange handling real-money positions.
DeepSigma’s broader ambition is to extend decentralized perpetual trading beyond cryptocurrencies, using infrastructure designed specifically for the pricing, liquidity, risk and regulatory requirements of traditional financial markets.

11,2026-10-13,"Meta's free cash flow fell 91% in the second quarter, and about $420B of its spending sits off the balance sheet
Mark Zuckerberg, co-founder, chairman, CEO, Meta Platforms
Mark Zuckerberg says Meta committed as much as $200B to AI infrastructure before anyone could tell him it would work, with his chief AI officer saying it was not clear at all. Meta’s free cash flow fell 91% in the second quarter.
Mark Zuckerberg says Meta committed to spending as much as $200B on AI infrastructure before anyone at the company could tell him it would work. He put the figure at $100B or $200B.
He asked chief AI officer Alexandr Wang whether he was sure, Bloomberg reported, and Wang’s answer was that it was not clear at all that it was going to work.
The candour has arrived in stages. In April, asked when the spending would return anything, Zuckerberg called it a very technical question.
Chief financial officer Susan Li kept warning that the cost of failure would be enormous. Zuckerberg told Wang that Li had scared him a little.
By July the answer had changed. He told staff that agentic development had not accelerated as expected, and that the bets had not come to fruition.
Revenue was not the problem. It rose 28% to $60.8B, while profit fell 14% and capital spending guidance moved to between $130B and $145B, against $72B last year.
Cash was. Meta’s free cash flow fell 91% in the second quarter, to $784M from $8.55B a year earlier.
Not all the spending sits on the balance sheet. A Nikkei study puts Meta’s off-balance-sheet debt at about $420B, with $27B of it attached to one Louisiana data centre.
Alphabet, by contrast, publishes a number. It disclosed $811B of contracted future spending at the end of June, up about $500B in a single quarter.
Meta has disclosed no comparable total. So the $100B or $200B Zuckerberg named on a podcast is his own figure, not one investors can check in a filing.
The IMF raised the same shape of risk this week. It called the revenue commitments behind these deals wrong-way risk, because the investment sours exactly when the promised revenue does.
Europe is exposed through the market rather than the spending. The fund says the euro area and Britain are less sensitive than advanced Asia, but hold far more US shares than before.
The next test is 21 October, when Meta reports third-quarter results after the close. The stock is up more than 12% this year, and fell 8% after the last report.

12,2026-10-13,"It is entering a market where the incumbent is British, valued at $4B, and raised twenty times Clueso's total funding in a single round
Clueso founders Prajwal Prakash, Akash Anand and Neel Balar.
Clueso has raised a $10M Series A led by Dharana Capital for AI agents that script, record and maintain corporate video, bringing total funding to $12M. The market it is entering is led by London’s Synthesia, which was valued at $4B in January after a $200M round.
Clueso has raised a $10M Series A for AI agents that make corporate video. Dharana Capital led the round, which takes the three-year-old company’s total funding to $12M.
More than 1,000 businesses use it, the company says, naming Atlassian, Zendesk, Seismic and Ironclad. It reports customer claims of fivefold productivity gains and 80% lower production costs, neither independently verified.
The market it is entering already has a leader, and the leader is British. Synthesia, in London, was valued at $4B in January after raising $200M in a round led by Google Ventures.
The gap is the story. Clueso’s entire funding history amounts to 6% of what its largest competitor raised in one go.
Clueso frames business video as underserved next to consumer tools. Synthesia says more than 60% of the Fortune 100 already use its platform, and has called its own product the first enterprise AI video platform.
One customer sits on both sides. Atlassian’s learning team is quoted praising Clueso, while Atlassian Ventures joined Synthesia’s funding round last year.
What Clueso does differently is capture. Its agents navigate real software, perform the workflow and record the footage themselves, then rerun it to refresh the video when the product changes.
It also opens the pipeline to other agents. A Clueso connector lets assistants including Claude and ChatGPT create, edit and manage videos directly.
The timing suits it. AI-native enterprise spending rose 94% year on year in the first quarter while traditional software grew 8%.
The round’s composition is unusual. Clueso says Dharana led, while more than 80% of the new money came from existing investors increasing their stakes.
That leaves the three new backers splitting under $2M of a $10M round. Y Combinator and f7 Ventures, the returning investors, would be supplying the rest.
The European money is French. Founders Future, which Marc Menasé founded in Paris in 2018 and which has written cheques of €250,000 to €3M, is among the new investors.
Dharana is India-focused and backed by US university endowments. Clueso itself is based in San Francisco and was founded in 2023 by three engineers who hit the problem at a previous company.
About Clueso
Clueso is an agentic video creation platform that helps teams across an organization create high-quality video at scale. A team can start with a product workflow, document, presentation, screen recording, or simply an idea for a video. Clueso can then automate much of the production process from understanding the context and writing the script to capturing footage, generating voiceovers and motion graphics, and editing everything into a polished video.
About Dharana Capital
Dharana Capital is an India-focused growth-stage technology and consumer investment firm. Dharana Capital’s current investments include Urban Company, LAT Aerospace, MyGate, Vyapar, Temple, Itilite, Petpooja, Vayudh and Beyond Appliances amongst others. Dharana is backed by top US University endowments and other non-profit institutions.

13,2026-10-13,"Pax8 asked 1,150 leaders in the US, UK, Germany and Sweden. It found shadow AI in 35% of firms using AI, and nearly half of AI buyers got a bigger bill than expected.
Small and mid-sized businesses have moved from buying AI tools to building their own. In every market surveyed, most do it without a formal, documented review, Pax8 found in a survey published on Monday.
Pax8 is a Denver-based marketplace. It sells AI and cloud software to small businesses through IT service providers. It surveyed 1,150 business leaders in the US, the UK, Germany and Sweden. The results form the Q3 2026 SMB AI Pulse Report. It is the third edition of the quarterly series, and the first to cover Europe.
“SMBs are moving from using AI tools to building AI into the way their businesses operate,” Nick Heddy, Pax8’s president and chief commerce officer, said in a statement. “The opportunity is significant, but so are the new demands around governance, security, cost, and accountability.”
Of the businesses that use AI, 67% have built at least one AI solution that is now in regular use. Another 19% are prototyping. Only 13% rely solely on off-the-shelf tools.
Among the builders, 52% are developing automated workflows that connect AI to other business systems. Close to half (46%) are building or testing AI agents that complete multi-step tasks on their own. Another 39% have built custom integrations using an AI provider’s API, and a third are customising or fine-tuning models.
The US leads, with 81% of businesses that use AI running solutions they built. The figure is 76% in Sweden, 54% in the UK and 52% in Germany. Sweden has the most builders working on AI agents, at 55%.
Last month, Meta launched Muse for small businesses, linking its AI agent to Shopify.
In the past six months, 35% of businesses that use AI discovered shadow AI. Pax8 defines it as tools, workflows or agents running without official approval. Sweden had the highest rate, at 55%. The US followed at 42%, then the UK at 24% and Germany at 19%.
Formal, documented review of AI built in-house ranges from 23% in the UK to 45% in Sweden. In the US, 36% of builders have formal review. Another 41% use review that is consistent but undocumented.
Policies on how staff use AI are more common. Some 40% of businesses have a documented policy and 35% have informal guidelines. Pax8 noted that such policies do not cover the agents and workflows a business builds itself.
Respondents named the risks of AI tools that employees build. In every market, data security or privacy exposure topped the list, cited by 30% to 35%. Last month, a Dataiku survey found that 81% of CIOs lack full oversight of AI agents built outside IT.
Of the businesses that paid for AI in the past year, 49% got a bill or usage charge higher than they expected. That rose to 57% in the US and 65% in Sweden. Germany was lowest, at 33%.
US businesses pay through usage-based pricing (46%), fixed subscriptions (41%) and per-seat pricing (40%). Pax8 said usage-based charges are the hardest to forecast. Some 69% said they would be more likely to expand AI with outcome-based pricing at a similar cost.
Pax8 also asked about data control. Seven in ten businesses would pay at least 6% more for greater control over how providers store and use their data. In the US, the figure was 81%.
Some 69% of businesses that use AI said it had freed up at least some capacity. But 55% of decision-makers personally spend three or more hours a week reviewing, editing or fact-checking AI-generated work, and 7% spend ten hours or more. In the US, 68% spend at least three hours.
Last month, a PwC survey of workers found most are falling behind on AI skills.
In all four markets, businesses that use AI extensively were more likely to feel ahead of competitors than those still experimenting. The gap was 63% against 40% in the US, and 80% against 24% in Sweden. The US is the only market where fear of falling behind (38%) outweighs fear of costly AI mistakes (32%).
Pax8 ran the survey online among owners, executives and managers involved in technology decisions at companies with 5 to 499 employees. It polled 400 in the US, 350 in the UK, and 200 each in Germany and Sweden.
The margin of error is about 2.9 percentage points for the full sample and 6.9 points for Germany and Sweden. Pax8 said results for smaller subgroups should be read as directional.
Pax8 works with more than 47,000 IT partners. Its report says the findings point to a growing role for partners that can help small businesses inventory, secure and govern the AI they build.

14,2026-10-13,"AI could help keep the global economy growing while pulling the first rung of the career ladder out from under young workers.
That’s the warning from International Monetary Fund managing director Kristalina Georgieva, who described AI as a “tsunami” for jobs during a conversation in Bangkok, where Thailand is hosting the IMF–World Bank Annual Meetings from October 12–18.
Georgieva told a panel on Monday that while AI investment is helping sustain global growth, a stronger economy doesn’t necessarily mean better career prospects, especially for young people.
""You have those that build the infrastructure for AI, and many of them are in Asia, including Thailand,"" Georgieva said. ""So that shift in higher gear is what is keeping the world economy growing at 3% — around 3% — despite the fact we are being dragged down by the energy supply shock.""
The IMF estimates that AI will affect about 40% of jobs worldwide, rising to 60% in advanced economies, with some jobs enhanced, others downgraded, and some eliminated.
""And when we look at this, what we recognize is that the people to worry about first and foremost are young people who are coming into the labor market,"" Georgieva said.
""Normally, they would get into these routine, repetitive jobs and then grow up the ranks,"" Georgieva said. ""But if they're gone, I don't think any young person wants to be a barista in a coffee shop, as you know, [their] professional future.""
LinkedIn executive Aneesh Raman has previously described the career ladder in the age of AI as a “climbing wall,” requiring sideways moves rather than linear promotions. An HR strategist has also recently told Business Insider that it's time to start hiring for soft skills and capabilities instead of whether someone could make a pivot table.
Georgieva said the government would need to rethink taxation and education as AI changes the job market.

15,2026-10-13,"ReadWrite's editorial staff consists of journalists, editors, and experts across iGaming & Technology. Over 20 years of combined experience and the latest in technology, AI,…
Six in 10 Americans oppose using prediction markets to wager on election results, a Reuters/Ipsos poll found, adding a public-opinion challenge as officials debate how to regulate the industry.
The online survey covered 4,506 US adults nationwide from September 30 to October 5, with a margin of error of about two percentage points. Opposition crossed party lines: 69% of Democrats and 61% of Republicans opposed election betting.
The poll also found that 65% opposed betting on US military actions and 63% opposed wagers on government policy announcements or decisions. Support was low for both categories, at 8% for military-action markets and 9% for policy markets.
Polymarket and Kalshi offer markets on elections and other real-world events, alongside sports. The companies say they have safeguards against misconduct and work with authorities, including referring accounts they consider suspicious.
The survey found that 93% of Democrats and 89% of Republicans considered it inappropriate for members of Congress or their staff to bet on political events. In April, the Senate barred senators, staff and other officers from using prediction markets, while Democratic lawmakers have introduced bills to restrict the industry. Those efforts reflect concerns that political-event markets could create opportunities for insider trading or manipulation, and that trading around government affairs could raise national-security risks. Lawmakers have also called for tighter prediction-market safeguards.
Views were less opposed, but still divided, on professional sports: 43% opposed prediction-market sports betting and 29% supported it. Three-quarters of respondents said wagering on real-world events through prediction markets is a form of gambling; 8% disagreed.
Polymarket has argued that its markets can provide information about events and said more than 90% of visits to its platform are to view markets rather than trade. The poll measures public sentiment, not whether the contracts are legally gambling or whether any particular market should be allowed.
The poll arrives as the Trump administration pursues federal rules for the industry and states challenge that approach. The CFTC asserts jurisdiction over the contracts as financial products, while state regulators argue that prediction markets facilitate gambling and should face state gaming laws.
The dispute over which framework applies could reach the Supreme Court, according to legal experts cited by Reuters. Until then, the poll adds a political measure of public resistance to a debate that will shape how operators such as Kalshi and Polymarket can offer event contracts in the US.

Tech > Gaming
The console release is still slated for next month, and a PC port might be coming sooner than anyone previously thought.
Writer
Writer
October 13, 2026, 5:22 pm ET
PC gamers might not have to wait much longer to play Grand Theft Auto 6 after all, according to a new report from GamesBeat. A new leak, attributed to four industry sources familiar with developer Rockstar Games’ plans, points toward a PC port for the highly anticipated game happening within the first three months of 2027.
Despite multiple high-profile delays, Grand Theft Auto 6 for the PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X/S is still on for a Nov. 19 release.
If the latest reporting is true, the leak is another headache for Rockstar and its parent company, Take-Two Interactive. The hype cycle for GTA 6 has been plagued by controversy, including the revelations that there will be no physical discs and that core features like certain car modifications and clothing shops will be locked behind the $100 “Ultimate edition” of the game.
More from CNET
A deluge of leaks from as far back as 2022 has made GTA 6’s development cycle shockingly transparent. The latest spoilers have been shared by a hacker group known as CyberLeek, which has continued to post new footage (and shill scammy cryptocurrency), despite the best efforts of Take-Two’s legal team.
In spite of the challenges Rockstar has faced in the lead-up to GTA 6’s release, analysts report blockbuster demand for the new game, with projections ranging from $3 billion to $5 billion in revenue during launch week.
A representative for Rockstar Games did not immediately respond to a request for comment.
Rockstar has historically ported its games to PC well after their initial console release. PC gamers waited nearly two years for an official Grand Theft Auto 5 port, and a little more than a year for a Red Dead Redemption 2 port.
Rockstar’s delayed PC ports are often attributed to a host of optimization and debugging challenges, especially because PC gamers run games on more diverse hardware. Some critics argue that the company “double-dips” on sales by creating FOMO and driving PC players to consoles first. When a port is released, the thinking goes, the PC player will buy a second company and bolster Rockstar’s sales.
Either way, there are several reasons why Rockstar might be trying to turn around this particular port more quickly than ever before.
If the development studio is planning to quickly release an online service for GTA 6, that would be a massive revenue driver – the current iteration of GTA Online rakes in more than $1 million each day, and has surpassed $5 billion in revenue over its lifespan.
If a new version of GTA Online is planned for GTA 6, having the wider base of PC players will likely be more profitable in the long term than any “double-dipping” sales practice.
Also, while consoles have always been the most profitable platform for Rockstar’s games, the market is shifting. The RAMageddon chip shortage is pushing hardware prices higher, and home consoles are becoming more expensive – and less accessible.
While these hurdles also affect folks looking to buy an off-the-shelf computer or build their own PC, the market share of PC gamers is growing nonetheless. Newzoo analysts predict that there will be more than 1 billion PC gamers by 2028.
Most PC game spending is focused on microtransactions – so if one were to release a massive live service game with the ability to purchase, say, virtual preloaded debit cards, there’s probably no better time to do so.

18,2026-10-13,"This article is part of 20 Years of 30 Rock, Slate’s celebration of the iconic sitcom two decades after its premiere.
There is no television series quite like 30 Rock. But—dry your tears—there are a surprising number of shows that share some of the NBC sitcom’s DNA, whether it’s the acerbic humor, the sheer density of jokes, the insider’s knowledge of pop culture and media, the delightful strain of surrealism, or the stock characters fleshed into so much more. Here to help you slake your 30 Rock thirst are Slate staffers’ recommendations for other series that are close enough, arranged in ascending order according to the somewhat arbitrary metric of “30 Rock-ish-ness.”

OK, hear me out: What if Liz Lemon (Tina Fey) were actually just an uptight lawyer and Jack Donaghy (Alec Baldwin) her philandering husband, and what if Jenna Maroney (Jane Krakowski) were actually a bisexual Punjabi investigator, and what if Tracy Jordan (Tracy Morgan) were Elsbeth Tascioni (Carrie Preston)? I know this CBS legal political drama technically has nothing to do with 30 Rock, and it isn’t even funny, and there’s virtually no crossover in either themes or characters. But can’t you kind of see it anyway? Besides, there are so many episodes of The Good Wife, and so many spinoffs—much like with the 30 Rock universe—that watching this series somehow presses on the same pleasure center. Plus, both shows somehow feature John Lutz, forever tying them together in infamy.  —Scaachi Koul
30 Rock-ish-ness: 4/10
I fully acknowledge that Succession and 30 Rock are pretty far apart on paper: One revolves around a workplace overseen by a conservative mogul who worked his way up from nothing, and who alternately terrorizes and coddles his flailing underlings, with whom he is personally entangled—oh, wait. But seriously, while these two series are doing vastly different things, there are flashes of I-can’t-believe-this-is-real-life absurdity in the HBO tragidramedy that certainly feel like something out of 30 Rock. Dubbing a sex pest “Mo Lester” and then trying not to let this fact slip while delivering a eulogy at said sex pest’s funeral? Youngest failson Roman (Kieran Culkin) watching from his phone with increasing dread as his very expensive rocket launch ends in an explosion? Eldest boy Kendall (Jeremy Strong) breaking out into a rehearsed rap performance as a birthday gift for his father, Jenna Maroney style? Yep, we here for you.  —Jenny G. Zhang
30 Rock-ish-ness: 5/10
If your favorite 30 Rock episodes are the spoofs and satires, get closer to the source with Comedy Central’s Strangers With Candy, the Amy Sedaris–led cult classic from the late ’90s. In a send-up of after-school specials of the ’70s and ’80s, Sedaris plays Jerri Blank, a self-described “boozer, user, and loser” who’s returning to high school after decades as a teenage runaway. Strangers With Candy is deeply weird, and can be a hard sell if you’re speaking to a normal person. But hey, where do you think Steve Buscemi’s “How do you do, fellow kids?” even came from, conceptually? That’s pure Jerri Blank, baby. You gotta respect your elders. —‍S.K.
30 Rock-ish-ness: 6/10 
Just one year before 30 Rock made it to air, another sitcom star put together a little-viewed showbiz comedy about how to make it in American TV—and how brutal that slog can be, especially for women. In HBO’s The Comeback, Lisa Kudrow, fresh off her iconic run as Phoebe in Friends, plays Valerie Cherish, a washed-up ’90s TV actress who finally gets a shot at a … comeback … as an older ensemble member in a new network sitcom titled Room and Bored. Her contract also entails that she agree to film a TV documentary about her return to Hollywood, providing the literal lens through which we view the inevitable. It’s a tough, smart, charming comedy that was perhaps a little too real for its moment, canceled after only one season thanks to low viewership. Appropriately, The Comeback earned a cult following and a second season in 2014—right after 30 Rock went off the air—which follows Valerie as she attempts to break into reality TV. A third and final season blessed viewers this year, forcing Valerie to navigate the disruptions introduced by generative A.I.  —Nitish Pahwa
30 Rock-ish-ness: 6/10
Is there, materially, a difference between Devon Banks (Will Arnett), the former vice president of West Coast news, web content, and theme park talent relations for NBC in 30 Rock, or GOB Bluth (Will Arnett), the eldest son of the Bluth family patriarch in Fox’s Arrested Development? Well, Devon is gay, and only GOB would try to return a dead dove to the pet store, but otherwise, they scratch a similar itch, and they even share an actor.
Arrested and 30 Rock have plenty of crossover when it comes to plotlines (high-achieving man with a mother who seems to hate him) and cast, and both shows are brutal and funny while finding room for periodical sincerity. In both series, family is the only thing worth anything, be it your real family or the people you see every day at work. And, finally, both seem to kind of hate the British, rightfully so. —S.K.
30 Rock-ish-ness: 6/10 
Given that it’s co-created by 30 Rock co-showrunner Robert Carlock, the new-ish NBC sitcom about a disgraced football star (Tracy Morgan) trying to revive his career with the help of a struggling documentary filmmaker (Daniel Radcliffe) is a descendent of 30 Rock in the most literal sense. But even without the shared creative DNA, its lineage would be established in the first episode, when Radcliffe’s character gets a gig at the University of Maryland Center for Documentary, Anime, and Pornography. (The running gag about a TV procedural called FDNY: Chicago, about New York firemen who fight fires in Chicago, is also a dead giveaway.) Although The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins is the first show Carlock has created without Fey—Sam Means, a writer for Unbreakable Kimmy Schmidt, Girls5eva, and Mr. Mayor fills her role—it’s such close spiritual kin to the adventures of Liz Lemon and Co. that it suggests Fey might have, over the years, gotten too much of both the credit and the blame. Although it doesn’t have the mother ship’s sustained zaniness, the series is a welcome return to Morgan’s wheelhouse, and perhaps the best showcase yet for Radcliffe’s substantial comedic skills. —‍Sam Adams
30 Rock-ish-ness: 7/10 
For a sitcom set mostly in a New York TV studio, 30 Rock bursts with inside-the-Beltway humor. Sometimes it’s a sly cameo or a knowing name-drop, but the show’s political jokes are rarely incidental. Its central relationship, after all, is between a career-oriented liberal feminist and a coiffed, George W. Bush–era Republican who spar over terrorism, Barack Obama, and other issues du jour—including in ways that now look prescient. (“Don’t get peer-pressured into invading Iran,” Liz sarcastically tells Jack before he leaves for a conservative confab in Season 2.)
Even so, some prefer their political satire with a few more bureaucratic bumblers, surrealist town halls, and send-ups of small-town pride. If that’s you, try Parks and Recreation, which overlapped with 30 Rock on NBC starting in 2009. The mockumentary-style series stars Amy Poehler as Leslie Knope, a sunny public servant in the titular city bureau of the fictional Pawnee, Indiana. Firmly a product of the Obama presidency, Parks and Rec softens 30 Rock’s bite to better showcase the optimistic possibilities of good governance. Its earnestness can sometimes turn cloying; in this case, the mentor–mentee relationship between liberal feminist Knope and her libertarian elder, Ron Swanson (Nick Offerman), changes him more than it changes her. But if you’re looking for a refuge from today’s headlines without leaving politics behind entirely, go visit Pawnee. When Parks and Rec does joke about Iran—also in Season 2—it seems content to leave things in the past.  —Ian Prasad Philbrick
30 Rock-ish-ness: 7/10
Before 30 Rock took us behind the scenes of a Saturday Night Live–style variety show, HBO’s The Larry Sanders Show gave viewers a peek behind the curtains of a network late-night talk show—the all-American, ubiquitous TV staple that stared down an existential crisis in the 1990s after Johnny Carson’s retirement. Comedian Garry Shandling, a Tonight Show regular, was offered his own late-night slot after Jay Leno was deemed Carson’s successor, but Shandling decided instead to produce a sitcom about making late-night television, where he’d portray the beleaguered, titular host. Despite its squarely ’90s setting, The Larry Sanders Show remains one of the most brilliant sitcoms in American history, a cynical yet heartfelt and painfully hilarious romp about the bullshit travails that go into America’s favorite nightly routines—one that, as a bonus, ropes in plenty of still-recognizable celebrities (Alec Baldwin, Henry Winkler) to portray bizarro doppelgangers of themselves. While nowhere near as joke-dense as 30 Rock, it was an undeniable influence on the NBC classic, and on countless other 21st-century comedies. —N.P.
30 Rock-ish-ness: 7/10 
The too-little-seen Girls 5eva is required viewing for any 30 Rock fan, especially anyone who counted themselves particularly captivated by “Werewolf Bar Mitzvah,” “Muffin Top,” and the NBC show’s many other musical-comedy highs throughout its seasons. After serving as a composer on 30 Rock, Jeff Richmond, who is, incidentally, Tina Fey’s husband, was given a bigger showcase for his talents on this three-season series about a bubblegum-pop-era girl group that tries to stage a comeback 20 years after their first run at fame.(The show was created by Unbreakable Kimmy Schmidt writer Meredith Scardino; Fey was an executive producer.) Richmond’s compositions, many with lyrics by Scardino, are hilarious, period-accurate, and genuinely catchy, especially as performed by cast members and actual singers Sara Bareilles and Renée Elise Goldsberry. Sadly, the show’s original home on a still-new Peacock made it hard for it to build the audience it deserved, and though Netflix swooped in and granted it a third season, it never caught on like it should have, and 5eva was cut tragically short. —Heather Schwedel
30 Rock-ish-ness: 8/10 
If Parks and Rec dials down 30 Rock’s cynicism about politics, a more recent spiritual successor elevates it into nihilism ad absurdum. HBO’s Veep (2012) stars Julia Louis-Dreyfus as the grasping but inept Vice President Selina Meyer in an imagined Washington populated by sycophants, ladder-climbing buffoons, and insult artists far too foulmouthed for network TV.
Like Parks and Rec before it, Veep shares more than comedic DNA with its predecessor. Louis-Dreyfus preceded Fey on SNL and even played Liz in some scene-stealing flashbacks on 30 Rock. But where Poehler’s Knope is dedicated and hypercompetent, Louis-Dreyfus’ Emmy-winning performance as Meyer mixes Liz’s sarcasm and Jack’s ambition with the cravenness of TGS writer J.D. Lutz. Veep insists that our politics are so morally void, nakedly transactional, and poorly planned that the dumbest possible explanation for any particular event is usually the correct one. That black heart made for excellent satire, even if it also sometimes created challenges for the show’s writers during Donald Trump’s first term. Yet, more than seven years after Veep ended, its irreverent gloss on the American government continues to hold up a much-needed funhouse mirror to a political era that has made some of us wonder whether the real thing is still OK to laugh at. —I.P.P.
﻿30 Rock-ish-ness: 8/10 
FX’s It’s Always Sunny in Philadelphia feels like what 30 Rock would have been if the writers didn’t get any craft services and instead were relegated to eating canned cat food and huffing glue. It’s Always Sunny, the longest-running American live-action sitcom, is much more arch than 30 Rock, and there are even fewer consequences in that South Philly universe than in Fey’s. But that same mean streak that the 30 Rock creator is famous for in all her work—Mean Girls remains indelible source material for understanding just how cruel and exacting teenage girls can be—is also present in Sunny, which follows the exploits of a group of friends running an Irish dive bar. This is a show that works best as an ensemble, much like 30 Rock did. Dennis (Glenn Howerton) is egotistical, Mac (Rob McElhenney) is weak, Charlie (Charlie Day) is stupid, Frank (Danny DeVito) is disgusting, and Dee (Kaitlin Olson) is ugly: All have their role, and it’s one of the best television troupes to continue on in 30 Rock’s shadow. —S.K.
30 Rock-ish-ness: 8/10 
After 30 Rock ended its run and it was time for Tina Fey to make something new, Netflix’s Unbreakable Kimmy Schmidt must have looked like quite a swerve: How do you make a sitcom about a woman who was kidnapped and imprisoned for 15 years? Fey proved that she could take a premise like that and turn it into a show that managed to feel just as fun, zany, and joke-packed as its predecessor, despite occasionally wrestling with violence and trauma. (Trauma—pronounced in the Jamie Lee Curtis manner—blessedly didn’t feel quite as cliché back in 2015 as it does now.)
One of the main differences between 30 Rock and Kimmy Schmidt might be the absence of a Liz Lemon straight-man type in the latter; instead, Ellie Kemper brought Kenneth the Page energy to the lead role, and supporting performances from Titus Burgess, Carol Kane, and Jane Krakowski added further sparkle, though some 30 Rock viewers might miss a world that felt a little more grounded in reality. The show’s standing seems to have faltered in recent years, probably due to it petering out a bit as it went on. (It also ended, somewhat confusingly, with an interactive film that is no longer accessible on Netflix.) Nevertheless, Unbreakable Kimmy Schmidt is still very much worth a watch. —H.S.
30 Rock-ish-ness: 9/10 
Tina Fey and Robert Carlock’s own efforts notwithstanding, there might be no show that better embodies 30 Rock’s spirit than Chris Kelly and Sarah Schneider’s showbiz satire, which debuted on Comedy Central and later migrated to HBO Max. Built around two thirtysomething siblings (Drew Tarver and Heléne Yorke) whose modest success becomes intolerable when their younger brother abruptly becomes a pop star, The Other Two skewers both the entertainment industry’s hypocrisy and the sweaty opportunism of the people desperate to maneuver their way to the top. The storyline where Tarver voices an animated green blob purported to be Disney’s first “unapologetically gay character” is a perfect send-up of both Hollywood’s meager stabs at representation and the actors who take endless victory laps for history’s smallest acts of bravery. The series ended abruptly and under a cloud in 2023, with Fey herself taking a public shot at its creators’ alleged toxicity. But that untimely demise just adds further weight to the show’s cautionary tales.  —S.A.
30 Rock-ish-ness: 9/10
The only television show that really gets close to 30 Rock’s joke-per-minute rate and slapstick visual goofiness would be, of course, a cartoon, and America’s longest-running cartoon, at that. Early seasons of The Simpsons are heavy on visual gags (“Nuts and gum: Together at last!”) and musical riffs, much like 30 Rock. To get to Jenna Maroney’s “Tennis Night in America,” we need “Canyonero,” The Simpsons’ fake jingle for a high-polluting SUV. To get to “Muffin Top,” we need every song performed by Phil Hartman’s Troy McClure in the Season 7 musical adaptation Stop the Planet of the Apes, I Want to Get Off!  —S.K. 
30 Rock-ish-ness: 10/10
Read the rest of 20 Years of 30 Rock.

19,2026-10-13,"

      Greg Rosalsky
    

This article first appeared in the Planet Money newsletter. You can sign up here.
On Monday morning, the world's econ nerds tuned in to an announcement from Sweden to learn who won this year's Nobel Prize in economics. And we got a lesson involving … ice cream.
The winners were Robert Hall of Stanford University and Michael Woodford of Columbia University. The prize committee honored them ""for microeconomic foundations of macroeconomic stabilization.""
To explain what the heck that means — as we will try to do in this newsletter — Timo Boppart, an economist at Stockholm University and member of the prize committee, stood up and walked around like he was giving a TED Talk. At one point, he brought up an image on the screen behind him: an ice cream menu.
Boppart used the ice cream menu to serve up a sweet lesson in economics.
""How do firms actually set prices?"" Boppart asked. "" This is illustrated here with this ice cream menu. Prices do not adjust [on] a continuous basis. Firms instead set the price of this ice cream for a whole season.""
Ice cream was a poetic example. Because prices, like ice cream, can get sticky. In fact, economists use the term ""sticky prices"" to describe how prices can stubbornly stay put even as economic conditions change.
That seemingly minor detail can have huge consequences for the entire economy. Sticky prices are a cornerstone of what's known as ""New Keynesian economics"" — a framework that has helped reshape macroeconomic theory and how policymakers think about fighting recessions and controlling inflation.
This year's prize honored work that helped lay the foundations for New Keynesian economics. In the process, Hall, Woodford, and other scholars helped breathe new life into the ideas of economics giant John Maynard Keynes, whose intellectual legacy, for a time, seemed in danger of melting away.
To understand why, we need to go back to the 1970s, when the economy experienced something that, for some, seemed to invalidate Keynesian economics.
John Maynard Keynes is basically the father of macroeconomics, the study of how the entire economy works, including what the government can do to fight recessions, boost employment, and control inflation.
Keynes' breakthrough came in the depths of the Great Depression, when he published The General Theory of Employment, Interest, and Money. He articulated an idea that may seem obvious today but challenged the economic orthodoxy of his time: an economy can get stuck in a prolonged slump, and the free market won't necessarily get everyone back to work on its own. Instead, Keynes wrote, we needed the government to help boost spending, put people back to work, and return the economy to health.
If you've ever taken an Econ 101 class, you've probably heard about the ""invisible hand"" of the free market. It's the idea that people and businesses pursuing their own interests in competitive markets can help the economy reach its full potential without the government having to step in. Keynes basically argued we couldn't count on the invisible hand to pull the economy out of a slump. We needed the visible hand of the government.
Keynes influenced an entire generation of economists and policymakers. Even Milton Friedman, a strong advocate for the free market, was quoted in 1965 as saying, ""We are all Keynesians now."" (He later said the quote was taken out of context.)
In the 1970s, however, the dominant Keynesian models — and the policies they helped guide — ran into trouble. Inflation and unemployment rose together — a painful combination known as ""stagflation."" The existing Keynesian models struggled to explain it.
""For a long time, the recipes of Keynes seemed to work, but they then failed during the oil crisis in the 1970s when high unemployment and high inflation took over simultaneously,"" said John Hassler, the chair of the Nobel committee, when they announced the prize.
When the old-school models faltered, some economists wanted to reject Keynes wholesale. Economists like Robert Lucas and Edward Prescott — who each won Nobel Prizes — challenged Keynesian economics and its confidence that government policy could effectively smooth out the economy's ups and downs. Call it the revenge of the invisible hand.
A central criticism: The Keynesian models relied too heavily on patterns found in past economic data without adequately explaining the individual decisions behind them. Addressing that criticism meant giving models ""microfoundations"": starting with the decisions of individual people and businesses to explain how the whole economy works. The economy is made of people, and people think ahead. They anticipate what policymakers will do, and those expectations shape how they spend, save, and invest. If policymakers change direction, people's behavior might too — making old patterns an unreliable guide to whether a new policy will work.
One influential alternative to Keynesianism, developed by Prescott and other economists, became known as ""real business cycle"" theory. These models assumed that prices and wages were completely flexible, adjusting freely as economic conditions changed. If demand for workers fell, wages could fall too, making workers cheaper to hire and allowing the labor market to balance supply and demand. That left little room for a familiar feature of recessions: people who want to work but can't find jobs.
Gregory Mankiw, now an economist at Harvard University, entered graduate school at MIT alongside Michael Woodford in 1980. They were learning macroeconomics just as economists were fighting over how to rebuild it.
""Lucas and Prescott were ascendant, but a lot of us were skeptical of their conclusions,"" Mankiw tells Planet Money. "" And that's when people like Woodford and Hall sort of entered the stage. The sense was that the people who were rejecting the Keynesian model were throwing out the baby with the bathwater.  And so rather than throwing it out, they wanted to sort of fix the problems.""
Hall and Woodford helped rebuild macroeconomics around the decisions of individual people and businesses. Hall broke new ground in understanding how people's expectations about the future shape their spending today. Woodford built on that work, developing a framework that combined forward-looking spending decisions with sticky prices.
To see why sticky prices matter, let's return to the ice cream example. Say there's a recession and demand for ice cream plummets. Shops might not quickly reduce ice cream prices to entice customers back. Maybe changing menus and signs costs time and money. Or maybe shops aren't yet sure whether the drop in demand is temporary or here to stay. Instead, they keep prices frozen, sell fewer scoops, and ultimately may end up having to cut worker hours or lay people off. When prices don't adjust, jobs and businesses take a hit.
The same story goes for wages, which we guess are a bigger factor in the economy than ice cream prices.
"" When economists talk about sticky prices, they're also including sticky wages — the price of labor,"" Mankiw says. ""Most people don't get their wages adjusted every month.""
Across the economy, that stickiness or ""price rigidity"" can have big consequences. When spending drops, prices and wages don't quickly and flexibly adjust to keep people buying and businesses hiring. Instead of prices falling to bring supply and demand together, in these models, price stickiness causes production and employment to fall. It helps explain why, during recessions, people who want to work can remain out of a job for long stretches.
"" Bringing in those price rigidities into the framework was absolutely key because this is the reason why the invisible hand cannot do its magic and there is room for policy intervention,"" said Boppart at the Nobel Prize announcement.
In other words, even when people and businesses think ahead and act in their own interests, sticky prices can keep the economy stuck in a slump. Call it Keynes' comeback. In these models, governments and central banks still have a role to play in getting the economy back on track.
This was the promise of New Keynesian economics: take the criticisms of the old models seriously, rebuild models around individual decisions, and preserve Keynes' central insight that the free market won't always bounce back on its own.
""One thing that clearly differentiates Keynesian models from Real Business Cycle models was the flexibility or stickiness of prices,"" Mankiw says. ""Most economists today — not all — but I think most economists today buy into the sticky price assumption.""
These New Keynesian models became integral to how governments and central banks think about fighting recessions and controlling inflation.
Woodford's work, for example, helped lay the theoretical groundwork for ""forward guidance,"" a strategy the Federal Reserve used during the Great Recession. The Fed slashed its benchmark interest rate to near zero, but the economy still needed stimulus. So under then-chair Ben Bernanke, the Fed signaled that it expected to keep interest rates low for an extended period. By shaping expectations about future rates, the Fed tried to help lower longer-term borrowing costs and encourage spending today.
Keynes' ideas survived with new foundations. And Mankiw offers an update to Milton Friedman's famous line: ""We're all New Keynesians now,"" he says.
P.S. Throughout this piece, we've called this award the ""Nobel Prize in economics."" But calling it that is surprisingly controversial. Technically, it has a much clunkier name. Economics wasn't one of the prizes Alfred Nobel created. So how did it join the club? Ricky and I tell that story in the latest episode of The Indicator from Planet Money. Check it out when it's released Tuesday.

20,2026-10-13,"JPMorgan Chase customers shouldn't expect their personal AI agents to have access to their accounts anytime soon.
The typical security concerns around any new adaptation of technology are ""heightened"" with AI agents, ""because you don't want rogue agents, and there could be thousands of them,"" JPMorgan Chase CEO Dimon said during an earnings call Tuesday. Unlike chatbots, which primarily answer questions, AI agents are designed to take action on behalf of a user and have increased in popularity after the introduction of Meta's personal AI assistant, Muse.
""If people are using them, they should understand the risk,"" Dimon said, saying there's a ""liability transfer"" away from the bank, as well as risks that their data is ""misused.""
The company is building its own agentic tools for clients, Dimon said, but for now, it plans to closely guard the bank from the onslaught of outside AI agents.
""In the meantime, we're not going to allow just any outside agent to come in inside the company because that's just too risky for clients and for JPMorgan,"" Dimon said.
The bank is already blocking access to ""to the extent that we're able to block those,"" Jeremy Barnum, the bank's chief financial officer, said.
""I think right now, some of the services that offer that do it through credential sharing, and we have a long record of being against that way of doing things, screen scraping, et cetera,"" he said. ""It's not secure.""
If a customer gives an AI agent their credentials, they could be responsible for any unwanted actions the agent takes.
""It's not an ideal state of things,"" Barnum said.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
JPMorgan has also tightened controls on AI agents used by its own employees. Business Insider previously reported that the bank rolled out new security measures for some engineers who use Anthropic's Claude, restricting AI's access to employees' internal systems.
Dimon and Barnum's comments were contrasted with concerns that AI agents could cause a bank run from low-yield into high-yield savings accounts, a theory raised by Apollo chief economist Torsten Sløk in late September.
Bank stocks, already declining following bond market turmoil, have fallen further since the launch of Meta's Muse on September 22, and Barnum said they saw ""a little bit of a response in bank stocks around those releases.""
Barnum suggested this concern is a continuation of two trends the bank has felt: ""yield-seeking flows"" and competition from fintechs.
As for yields in high-interest savings accounts, Barnum said the bank is focused on ""primary bank share"" rather than yield chasers. The bank's standard Chase Savings account earns a 0.01% annual percentage yield, while others in the market earn 4% (though it does offer higher-yield cash products to its private wealth clients).
And while Barnum said the bank works to ""have parity"" with fintech companies, he also thinks the bank's traditional brick-and-mortar footprint provides its own defense against encroachment.
""Our perspective is that, on the one hand, it's nothing new, but that doesn't mean that we're complacent about it,"" Barnum said about fintech competition.
