49,2026-10-13,"Visa first applied to China’s central bank six years ago. Mastercard and American Express already have approval, and Xi and Trump are due to meet in Shenzhen next month.
Visa has won approval to start preparing a domestic bank card clearing business in China, the company said on Tuesday. The People’s Bank of China approved its “Application to Prepare”, which lets Visa begin formal preparations. It does not yet let Visa process domestic payments.
Visa first applied to the central bank in May 2020, CNBC reported. Visa did not give a date for when it expects to start operating.
“We look forward to taking the necessary steps in the process, pursuant to the PBOC’s regulations, to begin operating domestically in China,” Visa chief executive Ryan McInerney said in a statement.
In the same statement, McInerney credited the leaders of the US and China for the relationship between the two economies.
McInerney joined President Donald Trump’s business delegation to Beijing in May, as Evelyn Cheng reported for CNBC. He became chair of the US-China Business Council in June. He led a council trip to Beijing in early September.
Trump told Fox News after the May trip that he had raised Visa’s request for market access, according to CNBC. After the September summit, China said it would approve applications from all foreign financial institutions to operate in the country.
The two governments have also opened talks on technology. In September, Xi told Trump the two countries could jointly prevent AI misuse. They later agreed an AI incident channel.
George Chen is a partner at The Asia Group. He told CNBC the approval came just before next month’s APEC leaders’ meeting in Shenzhen, where Xi and Trump are expected to meet.
“The move suggests that Beijing is keen to demonstrate tangible progress for American businesses before Trump’s next visit to China,” Chen told CNBC.
China has kept tight control of its financial sector and favoured domestic players. Visa’s main US rivals already have approval.
American Express was first. It received the central bank’s nod in 2018 and a processing licence in June 2020. Mastercard’s joint venture got approval to begin preparations in February 2020 and started processing payments in 2024.
Until now, Visa has worked with Chinese banks on dual-branded cards. China’s UnionPay processes those transactions at home, while Visa handles payments abroad.
Most everyday consumer payments in China run through mobile apps. The main ones are Tencent’s WeChat and Ant Group’s Alipay. Visa, Mastercard and Ant International teamed up last month on ID checks for agents that shop on behalf of people.

51,2026-10-13,"It's once again better to switch jobs than stay in your current gig if you want a raise.
""Wage growth for workers who change jobs is picking up again in 2026, even as raises for those who stay put keep slowing,"" Nicolas Petrosky-Nadeau, vice president for macroeconomic research at the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco, said in a LinkedIn post.
That's based on 12-month moving averages of median wage growth for job switchers and stayers tracked by the Federal Reserve Bank of Atlanta using Current Population Survey data.
Year-over-year wage growth for job switchers has been 4.4% for four straight months, while growth for job stayers falls short, with September's estimate at 3.5%. The gap between the two groups shrank last year, including a brief period where stayers actually saw slightly better wage growth than switchers. The recent trend could be the start of a return to the historical norm of a healthy job-switching premium.
""The first half of this year was the anomaly, and the low-hire environment is to blame,"" said Chris Martin, senior economist at Glassdoor. ""Job seekers had to take what they could get, and that often meant compromise: fewer promotions and lower wages.""
However, some workers don't have the luxury to switch jobs right now. The quits rate has been bouncing between 1.9% and 2% for a little over a year now, far below the 3% rate a few years ago during the ""Great Resignation,"" suggesting lower confidence among workers. Openings have been similarly sluggish and are substantially down from the March 2022 record of 7.5%.
""The return of this premium for job switchers is an early sign of improving prospects for job seekers, though the hiring rate remains low and job searches are taking longer,"" Martin said.
With job openings basically in line with the unemployment level, AI making it easier to send out résumés, and job losses in white-collar sectors, workers might find it tough to say goodbye to their current role.
""While job hopping still comes with higher pay, which is especially helpful for workers feeling the squeeze of inflation, the opportunities to job hop are few and far between,"" said ZipRecruiter economist Nicole Bachaud.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
People can improve their experience while stuck in their role. Bachaud suggests learning AI skills, which may be helpful in your current position or to note on your résumé.
""For workers who are feeling stuck in their current position, it's important not to fall into the complacency trap,"" Bachaud said. ""Beyond wages, the labor market is shifting rapidly as AI reshapes jobs.""
Whether you're applying for a new higher-paying job or seeking a raise from your current employer, it's not a bad idea to broadcast your accomplishments. People can ""loud work"" and boast their achievements and contributions to their manager and colleagues as well as beyond the workplace, such as through LinkedIn posts.
""We stop ourselves from self-promotion because we think it's bad, but self-promotion is the breadcrumbs that lead to the titles and the promotions and the pay raises,"" said Jasmine Escalera, a career expert with MyPerfectResume.
No comments right now, check back later.
Comments are unavailable right now.

Kyler Murray had the best throw of the week. Dak Prescott had some of the worst. 
Sam Darnold threw a pair of interceptions versus the 49ers, and Jayden Daniels took a sack at the end of overtime that ensured a tie against the Giants. 
Meanwhile, Jared Goff — of all people — is off to a historically prolific start for the Lions.
Let’s get into all of it.
These aren’t your typical QB rankings. I’m focused on contextualizing the fluctuations of starters' performances throughout the season. No one — not even Josh Allen — is safe at the top. No one — not even Malik Willis — is stuck at the bottom. There is room for quick ascent — and rapid decline. One question holds the most importance: What have you done for your team lately?

Cam Ward has failed to eclipse 200 passing yards four times in five games this season, and now people are questioning whether the Titans should bench last year's No. 1 overall pick. (Photo by Andy Lyons/Getty Images)

Previous ranking: 29 ↑
Daniel Jones has looked overwhelmed — all season — by what the Colts have asked him to do.
Their plan stunk from the beginning. I’ve been on record saying this season was destined for implosion. Unfortunately, I’ve been correct. On offense, it simply never made sense.
It’s one thing to return from his Achilles injury. It’s another thing to face expectations that — somehow — this offense would regain its form from the beginning of 2025 (which was already fading at the time of his injury). But the biggest issue is that the Colts have struggled to keep receiver Alec Pierce on the field because of injuries — and they traded away receiver Michael Pittman, a trustworthy possession receiver.
Without WR1 or WR2 from 2025, Jones has had to make the most of receiver Josh Downs, whose explosive plays aren’t enough to carry a whole offense. That’s not exactly a surprise. The surprise is that tight end Tyler Warren is averaging 7.1 yards per reception — struggling to find room to run after his 30 receptions, the eighth-most in the NFL.
There’s more that’s underwhelming. Running back Jonathan Taylor is averaging 3.9 yards per carry — on pace for his worst season and well behind his previous worst at 4.4 in 2023 when he played only 10 games. That’s in part because he’s faced the fifth-most stacked boxes (21) in the NFL. Colts opponents are stacking those boxes because they’re not afraid of Jones, especially when he doesn’t have Pierce to throw to.
The Colts' offense is functioning at an even lower level than you’d expect. In turn, so is Jones. In Week 5 against the Steelers — whose defense is good but beatable — Dimes couldn’t find the end zone. He was 24 for 44 for 267 yards. His completion percentage (CPOE) was -3.8 and was especially bad when the Steelers did not blitz him (-8.5%).
Defenses are daring him to pick them apart. He can’t do it.
Defenses are daring him to throw deep. He can’t do it.
Defenses are making Indy's middling starting QB look like a backup, which is especially frustrating for this franchise when considering Jones' cap hit in 2026 is just $19 million. Big changes are coming for the Colts, who don’t even have their 2027 first-round pick to look forward to. (They traded it to the Jets.)

I’ve been tracking Sam Darnold’s season, but for the most part, I thought he was about as bad (and good) as he’d been last year. But that’s not the case.
He’s worse. Much worse.
Through four appearances (and, really, just three, because he left Week 1 after only a few plays), he has 755 passing yards, seven touchdowns and five interceptions. The turnovers are a red flag, but … at least he’s posting production in yardage and scoring. Right? Well, the advanced stats tell a different story. Darnold has a -.19 expected points added per dropback, which is a tough fall from a much healthier .07 in 2025. 
The loss to the 49ers emphasized Darnold’s struggles with inconsistency and turnovers. The team around Darnold also felt different.
Last year, the Seahawks figured out how to win games like this past Sunday. They’d do it despite Darnold. During their postseason run, they hit the sweet spot where Darnold was playing the best football of his life — and it meshed perfectly with ascending superstars Jaxon Smith-Njigba and Kenneth Walker III and an elite group of defensive playmakers. 
Without Walker this year, the running game hasn’t supported Darnold with shorter second and third downs. So Darnold is facing longer odds at conversions, and he’s forcing it, leading to more mistakes and more turnovers. 
It’s making me think that maybe Darnold is something of a Joe Flacco. 
Both QBs strung together a memorable playoff run when they caught fire and played at a level they’d never reached before. But the work before and the work after? That’s all fallen somewhere in a range of slightly below average and slightly above average. And for what it’s worth, Darnold also shares Flacco’s propensity for trusting his arm too much — and forcing the football into turnover-worthy plays and interceptions.
Maybe Darnold finds his 2026 postseason form again — and this current lapse is a lingering issue from the Week 1 hip injury. But it’s more likely that this is who Darnold is. The 3-2 Seahawks, whose next three games are against the Broncos, the Chiefs and the Bears, will have to figure out how to get back to working around their QB.

Previous ranking: 15
Previous ranking: N/A
Jayden Daniels' performance in Week 5 was a reminder of how young he is as a pro football player. Maybe too young, if you ask me. Maybe immature on a few levels. But it’s important to remember this was just his 30th appearance in the NFL, given all his injuries. He hasn’t played two full seasons yet. 
That won’t make the Commanders’ final play make any more sense. But it does add context to their fiasco in overtime, where they let time expire after Daniels took a sack to let time run out. 
We know, per coach Dan Quinn, that the fateful play was designed for an outside throw (where the pass-catcher can get to the sideline and stop the clock) or a clock-stopping throwaway.
Quinn took blame for even calling that play when they didn’t need to gain any more yards. But his revelation — ""outside throw or a clock"" — is an indictment of Daniels’ situational awareness. Because he ran to the left and took a sack near the line of scrimmage. Did he think he had a timeout and was he placing the ball on the left hash? I’m not sure. But the quarterback was clearly confused about the situation.
""We’ve gotta stop the clock,"" Daniels said. We know the situation. We’ve gotta relay that as an offense. It starts with everyone — top down. Ultimately it’s on me,"" 
It’s overtime. You need to know how many timeouts you have. Period. Most high school quarterbacks have more situational awareness than Daniels demonstrated. 
It’s good that he concluded by saying it’s on him. Because before that, he vaguely pointed his fingers at the coaching staff — perhaps for not reminding him that he didn't have a timeout left. It reveals two points of immaturity: 1) Daniels needs reminding of timeouts (and general hand-holding) at the game’s most crucial moments, and 2) Daniels isn’t wise enough to simply take the public blame.  
His comments have, no doubt, accelerated coach Dan Quinn’s departure.
The ending sequence marred what was otherwise an impressive game from Daniels. He had 300 total yards (199 passing; 103 rushing) and two rushing touchdowns. He had the most scramble yards of his career, per Next Gen Stats. And Daniels hit 20.54 mph on his 38-yard TD run, the fastest play of his career.

Previous ranking: 24 ↑
Kyler Murray had the best throw of the week, given the stakes and the risk and the … everything.
The Vikings were trailing by a point in the third quarter when Murray stood in a clean pocket and fired the ball into the deep left corner of the end zone. There was Justin Jefferson, of course. It’s basically always a sensible decision to throw to him. But standing all around JJ, there were three defenders. It was double coverage with a third Saint running over to help in any way possible. He couldn’t. New Orleans was beyond help.
Murray put the ball in a perfect spot — just beyond the defenders' reach. And Jefferson elevated to snatch the ball. The margin for error is always small when throwing in an area with so many defenders. But it’s even smaller when dealing with the sideline and the back of the end zone.
This was the throw that should worry folks around the NFL.
So far this season, Murray has been the ultimate game manager. He demonstrated restraint in all the ways the Vikings hoped he would (and all the ways Murray seemed to feel he couldn’t afford in Arizona, where he was constantly trying to do it all).
Murray went 24 of 30 (80% completion rate and a +6.8 CPOE) for 229 yards and a touchdown. He also had 10 carries for 51 yards (+15 RYOE).
In other words, he’s quietly helping Kevin O’Connell get the most out of this Vikings offense. 
It seems that, together, Kyler and O’Connell are getting their groove back.
Previous ranking: 6 ↓
There's been plenty of drama around a Cowboys staffer telling Dak Prescott that the team’s loss to the Buccaneers this past Thursday was ""embarrassing."" That wasn’t how I saw it.
Sure, Prescott was no revelation — nor did he redeem his team for its terrible performance. But he was fairly steady in a game where his pass-catchers were struggling with separation — and having difficulty wrangling the contested catches that basically define this offense’s success. 
The Cowboys had three drops against Tampa Bay, per PFF.
Both of Prescott’s interceptions went off the hands of a receiver — and on well-thrown balls. You’ll hear people talk about how sacks are a quarterback stat. (Which is about 75 percent true.) And you’ll hear people asking that some interceptions get scored against receivers. Well, these two picks were perfect examples of why.
Dak finished with a 57% completion rate, but PFF scored him an adjusted completion rate at 67.5. That includes the drops and other on-target throws that should’ve been completions.
This doesn’t totally excuse Prescott for struggling with ball placement. He should’ve been sharper, leaving meat on the bone when throwing to George Pickens. Yes, believe it or not, Pickens’ big day (nine catches, 130 yards and a TD) should’ve been even bigger. 
Prescott's M.O. is pinpoint accuracy, and he can’t blame all the incompletions on his receivers. I just can’t fault him too deeply for a night where his teammates and coaches were the ones making the biggest mistakes.

Jared Goff is now at 12 passing touchdowns and zero turnovers for the first five games. He’s the first QB in NFL history to do that. He’s leading the NFL in passing yards and is tied for second in passing touchdowns. And his connection with Jameson Williams finally clicked in this game — which I’d been saying for weeks was a reason why this offense could actually get better.
It did get better — with Williams and Goff making up for lost time and missed opportunities. They connected on two touchdown passes, including one from 39 yards out in the Lions' shellacking of the Cardinals.
The one point of context from the first five weeks: the Lions have faced the worst scoring defense (Cardinals), second-worst (Saints), fifth-worst (Bills) and seventh-worst (Panthers). The Jets are the best unit the Lions have faced, and to the latter's credit, they put up 31 points against New York’s stingy defense, which is allowing an average of 19.2 points per game (fifth-best).
Amid this quietly historic run, Goff is making small improvements around his career-long difficulties with pressure. When facing pressure, he has an EPA per dropback of -.19. Without pressure, he has an EPA per dropback of .45. The former mark is still pretty bad. But this represents a better split than it has been from 2023 to 2025, when Goff saw the biggest drop-off in the NFL (falling from .43 without pressure to -.42 against it). It’s also a heck of a lot better than Michael Penix, who falls from .63 unpressured to -.82 pressured.
Goff will spend his season contending with his defense — which is among the worst in the league. That’s his most obvious problem. But the Lions also won't go anywhere until their veteran QB figures out how to handle and neutralize pressure, both with pre-snap adjustments and with post-snap poise. That’s the final frontier for Goff. Of course, it has been for quite some time.

12 October 2026


Samsung Apple Xiaomi Oppo vivo Android iOS

Counterpoint Research (CR) has just published its report regarding the global smartphone market for the third quarter of the year (July to September). And it was a terrible one. Shipments dropped 20% compared to Q3 2025. The only winners in terms of growing their market shares were Samsung and Apple.
Samsung led the pack with a 23% market share (compared to 19% in the year-ago quarter), while Apple was a close second with 22% (up from 18% in Q3 2025). They were followed by Xiaomi with 10% of the pie (down from 14%), Oppo with 9% (down from 12%), and vivo with 8% (down from 9%).
CR says Q3 2026 saw the lowest smartphone shipments in 14 years in a third quarter, and of course it's all due to ""continued memory shortages"" as CR puts it. We'd qualify that a bit - it's not shortages per se, but rather a shortage of not-insanely-priced memory.
Anyway, Samsung's decent performance is apparently due to ""robust early demand"" for the Galaxy Z Fold8, Fold8 Ultra, and Flip8 (although the Fold8 was apparently the king of the bunch). On the other hand, Apple's market share crossed 20% for the first time in any Q3 in history. CR says premium device demand remained resilient, while the affordable end of the market is being squeezed out by increasing prices thanks to the ever more expensive memory.
Global retail smartphone prices have gone up by 20% so far this year, CR says. The firm thinks the outlook for the rest of the year ""remains challenging"", with smartphone makers increasingly prioritizing profitability over shipment volumes. Production cuts, trimmer portfolios, and a greater focus on premium devices are all expected to continue.
Source
They are always the winners. Doesn't mean they are the best. The best is what you love and you have. So if my family loves iPhone I love Samsung. But have other branded devices as well.
If a company sold fewer phones, then fixed expenses were applied to a smaller number of products. So they made less profit on each phone. On top of making less revenue from selling fewer products. Less profit = better?
They always had lag and always will , whatever the specs.

What do influencing, AI and health sciences have in common? They're all degree programs colleges have launched in the last few years to attract students looking for a new career path.
AILSA CHANG, HOST:  Colleges across the country are adding new majors that they hope will attract new students and train them for the future. Our colleagues, Wailin Wong and Ricky Mulvey, over at The Indicator from Planet Money tell us how colleges are creating programs for a changing U.S. job market.WAILIN WONG, BYLINE: Eddie Hernandez is a professor in human anatomy and physiology at UT San Antonio. His students call him Dr. H, so we will too. Dr. H said conversations about a new health sciences degree program started a few years ago.EDDIE HERNANDEZ: We have a fantastic biology degree, but it seemed a little broad, and it also seemed like it didn't reflect our specific population of students.RICKY MULVEY, BYLINE: Students like Case Brown - a few months ago, Dr. H told Case, guess what? The college has this new major. It checks off all the pre-recs that you need for medical school. Plus, you'll get more tailored courses that will prepare you for an actual career as a doctor.CASE BROWN: Dr. H came. He was like, hey, Case, this is new. This is how it'd benefit you. I was like, cool, sign me up.MULVEY: Dr. H says by his last count, there are around 500 undergraduates in the current cohort.WONG: And he says that if these future doctors and physicians assistants stay in Texas to build their careers, that will be another win for the program.MULVEY: And sustainable careers are the ultimate goal of these new degrees. That puts programs without a clear pathway to a job on the chopping block.WONG: The last decade or so has already been hard for the liberal arts in the U.S. The number of bachelor's degrees awarded in humanities is down 30% since 2012. That's according to the American Academy of Arts and Sciences.MULVEY: Jason Fox is the chief academic officer at the Savannah College of Art and Design in Savannah, Georgia. Jason's college has art right there in its name, and yet it still has to make tough calls about what programs to keep. This year, the college stopped accepting new enrollees in sculpture. Jason says the decisions come down to jobs and earnings potential.JASON FOX: These are economic decisions as much as they were - either, A, we weren't delivering on the promise, or B, there was no promise to deliver on because there was no market out there for it.WONG: On the flip side, Jason and other administrators at SCAD realized a few years ago that there would be a huge market for college graduates with training in AI.MULVEY: Jason started talking to people at both SCAD and in industries like film and fashion.WONG: So in September of 2025, SCAD launched a new major in applied AI. NVIDIA came on board as a corporate partner.MULVEY: The school says the program should equip students for careers in designing AI products and machines. Jason says the No. 1 metric that the college looks at is the employment rate of graduates, and it's hard to justify programs that don't lead to sustainable careers.FOX: If you're offering generalized degrees, especially in a specialty university like ours, it begs the question, OK, what am I going to do next? And at the undergraduate level, I don't think education consumers today are comfortable with that.MULVEY: Education consumers - now, that's an interesting term, but it makes sense given the escalating cost of higher education. Students and their families want a monetary return on investment, and SCAD isn't alone in adding AI to its roster of degrees. There are around 80 AI majors being offered in the U.S. right now, according to a tracker from Northeastern University.WONG: But all is not lost for the liberal arts. UCLA has a new department of digital humanities. The school says students will use digital tools to explore what it means to be human.MULVEY: Ricky Mulvey.WONG: Wailin Wong, NPR News.(SOUNDBITE OF MINUTEMEN'S ""COHESION"")

I was like, cool, sign me up.MULVEY: Dr. H says by his last count, there are around 500 undergraduates in the current cohort.WONG: And he says that if these future doctors and physicians assistants stay in Texas to build their careers, that will be another win for the program.MULVEY: And sustainable careers are the ultimate goal of these new degrees. That puts programs without a clear pathway to a job on the chopping block.WONG: The last decade or so has already been hard for the liberal arts in the U.S. The number of bachelor's degrees awarded in humanities is down 30% since 2012. That's according to the American Academy of Arts and Sciences.MULVEY: Jason Fox is the chief academic officer at the Savannah College of Art and Design in Savannah, Georgia. Jason's college has art right there in its name, and yet it still has to make tough calls about what programs to keep. This year, the college stopped accepting new enrollees in sculpture. Jason says the decisions come down to jobs and earnings potential.JASON FOX: These are economic decisions as much as they were - either, A, we weren't delivering on the promise, or B, there was no promise to deliver on because there was no market out there for it.WONG: On the flip side, Jason and other administrators at SCAD realized a few years ago that there would be a huge market for college graduates with training in AI.MULVEY: Jason started talking to people at both SCAD and in industries like film and fashion.WONG: So in September of 2025, SCAD launched a new major in applied AI. NVIDIA came on board as a corporate partner.MULVEY: The school says the program should equip students for careers in designing AI products and machines. Jason says the No. 1 metric that the college looks at is the employment rate of graduates, and it's hard to justify programs that don't lead to sustainable careers.FOX: If you're offering generalized degrees, especially in a specialty university like ours, it begs the question, OK, what am I going to do next? And at the undergraduate level, I don't think education consumers today are comfortable with that.MULVEY: Education consumers - now, that's an interesting term, but it makes sense given the escalating cost of higher education. Students and their families want a monetary return on investment, and SCAD isn't alone in adding AI to its roster of degrees. There are around 80 AI majors being offered in the U.S. right now, according to a tracker from Northeastern University.WONG: But all is not lost for the liberal arts. UCLA has a new department of digital humanities. The school says students will use digital tools to explore what it means to be human.MULVEY: Ricky Mulvey.WONG: Wailin Wong, NPR News.(SOUNDBITE OF MINUTEMEN'S ""COHESION"")

56,2026-10-13,"Michael Burry has long been bearish on AI, and his latest big short is evolving as the trade continues to prop up the stock market. 
Burry, famous for betting against the US housing ahead of the 2008 crash, earlier this year revealed short positions on winning stocks like Nvidia and Palantir. On October 11, he detailed how the bearish bet has evolved lately. 
""I had previously rolled my short positions into options positions,"" Burry wrote on his a Substack, unpacking his thesis under a section title ""Consolidating the Big Short."" ""Now I have rolled most of my options positions into two index short - puts on the NDX and puts on the Van Eck Semiconductor ETF (SMH).""
Burry's bearish bets against chip stocks over the summer likely paid off as the sector was pummeled by valuations concerns and other bearish headwinds. Now he's focused solely on two broad baskets that track soaring AI stocks. 
Burry said that he sees a ""significant top"" in the market sometime in the coming nine months, and that the final stretch of the bull run could be ""wild,"" with a potentially rapid run-up in prices before the pullback he's eyeing. 
The VanEck Semiconductor ETF ""is a concentrated bet on the hottest and most overextended semiconductor stocks,"" he wrote. 
""Nvidia (NVDA) is 19.6% of the fund, with Taiwan Semiconductor (TSM) at 9.3%, Advanced Micro Devices (AMD) at 5.6%. Broadcom, Micron, Marvell and Applied Materials are all near 5% positions,"" Burry. ""This is very much in my wheel house of individual names I would like to be short.""
He also said that the bearish puts he's purchased expire in June 2027, giving roughly eight months for his thesis to be proven correct. 
Burry is still betting againt some names directly. He said he still owns  puts on hot AI stocks inclduing Oracle and Palantir, as well as Met Life and Caterpillar.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

57,2026-10-13,"Under an ‘only once’ rule, companies would submit information a single time and AI would help authorities share it.
European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen delivers the 2026 State of the Union address at the European Parliament in Strasbourg.
Ursula von der Leyen wants companies in the EU to provide officials with the same details just once, with AI helping public bodies use them. The European Commission president made the pitch on Monday, Politico reported, citing a copy of her speech.
She spoke at a closed-door gathering of the European People’s Party, her centre-right political group, in Taormina, Sicily.
Businesses waste time reporting the same details again and again to different authorities, Ursula von der Leyen argued, and neither they nor Europe can afford that.
“And this is why we will introduce ‘Only Once’, to report data only once – and with the help of AI the administration will ensure efficient use,” she said, according to Politico.
It is the first time she has openly proposed using AI to lighten the paperwork companies face.
The idea of reporting only once is not new, though, as the Commission already built it into its EU Inc. proposal in March. That plan would create a single company structure that works across all member states.
Under EU Inc., firms could file their details once through an EU-wide online portal, and authorities would share the data among themselves. That plan, however, does not mention AI.
Von der Leyen also gave no details on how an AI-based system would work, which companies it would cover, or when the Commission plans to introduce it.
In the same speech, she urged national governments to sign a pact on gold-plating. That is the practice of adding additional national rules when transposing EU laws into national law.
“A rule agreed at European level cannot become more complex when transposed or implemented nationally,” she wrote in a letter to EU leaders on Monday, Politico reported.
Under the pact, each government would have to list and explain any rules it adds on top of EU law. It would also have to assess how those rules affect businesses and the single market, according to the European Commission.
The push is part of the Commission’s wider effort to cut red tape, which has already reopened digital laws such as the GDPR and the AI Act.

60,2026-10-13,"The platform already runs in BMWs, Porsches and Golfs, so one supplier now sits in the dashboards on both sides of Europe's EV contest
AirConsole is coming to Zeekr, its first partner inside Geely Auto Group, with a demonstration at the Paris Motor Show before a rollout in select models by the end of 2026. The same platform already runs in more than 5 million BMW, Volkswagen, Audi, Porsche, Škoda and Tata vehicles.
AirConsole is going into Zeekr cars. It is the in-car gaming platform’s first partner inside Geely Auto Group, and the companies are showing it at the Paris Motor Show this week.
The platform already runs in more than 5 million vehicles, its maker says, across BMW, Volkswagen, Audi, Porsche, Škoda and Tata. Zeekr is the first Chinese marque on that list.
It got there through Europe first. BMW signed in 2023 and Volkswagen followed in 2024, putting games on the infotainment screens of ID models and Golfs while parked.
The company behind it is described as Swiss, which is half the picture. N-Dream is based in Zurich, but KPIT Technologies, listed in Pune, raised its holding from 26% to nearly 90% a year ago.
So the supplier is Indian-owned, Swiss-run, and now sells to German, Czech, Indian and Chinese carmakers at once. The software layer is consolidating faster than the cars around it.
Zeekr itself is no longer independent either. Geely completed its buyout and took the brand off the New York Stock Exchange last December.
The timing is not accidental. Chinese brands passed 15% of Europe’s EV sales in April, and EU tariffs of 17% to 38% have slowed that only marginally.
Zeekr is selling itself as a European product. It has research centres in Sweden, Germany and the UK, a design studio in Gothenburg, and brought its full European line-up to Paris.
Its parent is going further. Geely has said it will stop building factories and use Volvo’s instead, which puts Chinese-designed cars inside the tariff wall.
The demonstration itself is modest. AirConsole runs in the 7GT’s infotainment system, and visitors use a phone as a controller after scanning a code on screen.
The headline game is PAC-MAN Championship Edition, licensed through Bandai Namco. The cabin lighting reacts to what happens on the display.
Anthony Cliquot, chief executive of N-Dream, said the deal signals where the next wave of in-car entertainment is coming from, naming a generation of carmakers treating infotainment as core rather than an extra.
The rollout follows before the end of the year, in select models, bundled into the Zeekr Connected Premium package rather than sold separately.
About AirConsole by N-Dream
Drive together, play together. N-Dream is the in-car experience company behind AirConsole, the world’s ﬁrst gaming platform speciﬁcally designed for cars. AirConsole’s immersive features transform your car into a fully featured gaming station, integrating the infotainment, sound, and light systems into a single entertainment experience. AirConsole’s rich portfolio of games is tailored to casual players of any age, and by allowing any passenger to join using their phone as a controller, it is a true social experience. Available across premium OEM partners including BMW, Volkswagen, Audi, Porsche, Škoda, and Tata Motors, N-Dream is headquartered in Zurich, Switzerland, and is a subsidiary of KPIT Technologies.
About Zeekr
Zeekr is the global premium mobility brand from Geely Auto Group, combining the power of a global automotive giant with a European soul. Zeekr aims to create a fully integrated user ecosystem with innovation as a standard. The brand utilizes Sustainable Experience Architecture (SEA) and develops its own battery technologies, battery management systems, electric motor technologies, and electric vehicle supply chain.
Zeekr operates its R&D centres and design studios in Gothenburg, Ningbo, Hangzhou, and Shanghai and boasts state-of-the-art facilities and world-class expertise. Since Zeekr began delivering vehicles in October 2021, the brand has delivered more than 900,000 vehicles to date. Zeekr has an ambitious roll-out plan over the next 5 years to satisfy the rapidly expanding global EV demand. Zeekr Europe is active in Sweden, Norway, Denmark, the Netherlands, Belgium, Switzerland, Greece, Croatia, Slovenia, Romania, Bulgaria, Germany, Italy, Spain, Portugal, France, Finland, Lithuania, Estonia, Latvia, Poland and Austria.
About Bandai Namco Entertainment Inc. / PAC-MAN Championship Edition
PAC-MAN Championship Edition was developed by Bandai Namco Entertainment, reimagining the arcade classic PAC-MAN with modern gameplay mechanics.

Here’s a statistic that might keep you up at night: As many as 83 million U.S. adults suffer from obstructive sleep apnea, a condition that causes them to stop breathing over and over again while they sleep, waking up dozens or even hundreds of times a night.
The standard treatment is a CPAP machine, a clunky device that keeps the airway open by delivering pressurized air through a mask. It works. But millions of people with sleep apnea remain untreated.
For entrepreneur Wesley Lones, the numbers didn’t add up.
“I spent two years looking over my shoulder thinking, how could the most advanced wealthy country in the history of the world have millions of people suffering from an ailment and not have a treatment,” Lones says. “I must be missing something.”
What Lones eventually realized was that the problem wasn’t the lack of an effective treatment. It was the treatment itself. Many patients simply hate sleeping with a mask strapped to their face while pressurized air blows into their nose or mouth.
He wasn’t looking to start a new business. After helping build Byte, which was acquired for $1.04 billion, Lones was in the fortunate position of not having to work. He planned to take a few years off. “That lasted about seven minutes,” he says.
The more he studied the problem of sleep apnea, the harder it became to ignore the opportunity. Lones founded Daybreak, a fully remote platform that takes patients from diagnosis through treatment at home and offers a custom-fit oral appliance as an alternative to CPAP. The company has treated more than 20,000 patients in roughly its first 28 months, according to Lones. 
On a recent episode of One Day with Jon Bier, Lones talked about spotting a hole in a massive healthcare market, building Daybreak without outside funding and why his biggest challenge may be making millions of people aware they have sleep apnea and that an alternative treatment exists.

One of the biggest misconceptions about sleep apnea is that it’s a weight problem. Lones points out that anatomy can play a major role. When you sleep, the muscles around the airway relax, and for some people their anatomy makes the airway more likely to narrow or collapse, interrupting their breathing. Weight can make the problem worse, but it isn’t necessarily the cause.
Lones has nothing against CPAP as a treatment. “It’s the gold standard,” he says. “I’m not here to demonize it.” But he also knows there are entire Facebook support groups devoted to coping with one. 
One alternative is a custom-fit mouthguard that gently moves the lower jaw forward to help keep the airway open while you sleep. Oral appliance therapy wasn’t new. Dentists and sleep specialists have been using the devices for years. Most people just don’t know about them.

Lones investigated why oral appliances weren’t reaching more patients. The problem, he concluded, was a lack of awareness. 
“I have yet to interact with a person who has sleep apnea and hears about an oral appliance and says, ‘Wow, I would never try that,’” Lones says. “The universal, consistent answer is, ‘I had no idea that exists, and I would absolutely try it. How do I get started?’
The answer to that question became Daybreak. Patients are mailed a small wireless sleep-test device that wraps around a finger and tracks their sleep at home. The data is sent to a doctor for review and diagnosis. If they qualify for treatment, Daybreak sends them an impression kit used to create their custom-fit oral appliance. The entire process can be done without stepping into a doctor’s office. 

Lones built Daybreak without outside funding. Part of the reason is that his previous exits gave him a head start. But the economics of the business also helped. Because every oral appliance is custom-made, Daybreak doesn’t have to manufacture large quantities of finished products and hope they sell. For cash customers, the company collects payment upfront and then sends the order to the manufacturer. “It’s a reverse sort of cash cycle where we don’t have to carry a bunch of inventory,” Lones says. 
Bootstrapping has also allowed him to move quickly. “We have a lot of flexibility,” Lones says. “We have the ability to iterate, move very quickly, really compress the time between when we identify, learn there’s a problem, and then react to that problem and solve it.”

As Daybreak began treating patients, Lones started seeing the impact firsthand. One of the stories that stuck with him came from a mother of three whose husband suffered from sleep apnea. After his first night using the device, she contacted Daybreak with a message: “Thank you. This is the first time my children have had a father and I have had a husband in three years.”
“That’s why I say that it started to me as a business problem. I thought, ‘I think I can solve this.’ Then it became a mission because I started to see the impact.”
Here’s a statistic that might keep you up at night: As many as 83 million U.S. adults suffer from obstructive sleep apnea, a condition that causes them to stop breathing over and over again while they sleep, waking up dozens or even hundreds of times a night.
The standard treatment is a CPAP machine, a clunky device that keeps the airway open by delivering pressurized air through a mask. It works. But millions of people with sleep apnea remain untreated.
For entrepreneur Wesley Lones, the numbers didn’t add up.

We'll be in your inbox every morning Monday-Saturday with all the day’s top business news, inspiring stories, best advice and exclusive reporting from Entrepreneur.

62,2026-10-13,"A shot in the dark.
Sony today announced a new version of its disc-less PlayStation 5 console. The console is getting a Midnight Black variant but it's sadly only been announced for the Japanese market.
As revealed on the Japanese PlayStation Blog, the Midnight Black PS5 Digital Edition launches on 19th November (yep, that's GTA 6's release date) for ¥55,000 (~$380 / £263).
If that price sounds cheap, it's because the new console is based on the region-locked PS5 Sony launched in Japan last November. The Digital Edition console's language cannot be changed from Japanese, and it only works with PSN accounts based in Japan, preventing it from being exported outside the country - hence the cheaper price.
Sony is also releasing a Midnight Black blu-ray disc drive to go along with the new console, for players who want to retain access to their physical media - for however long that's going to be worth it. Maybe the introduction of this new model will relieve the pressure Sony is facing with its PS5 Pro - a console so in-demand in Japan that the company has had to implement a lottery system in order to adequately effectively distribute supplies.
While the rest of the world does not have a Midnight Black PS5, you can replicate the look by buying the Midnight Black Console Covers, which cost $54 / £45. The PS5's covers can be easily removed and replaced. Seeing as the middle of the PS5 is already black, purchasing and installing these covers achieves the same effect, if you're so inclined. If you want to complete the collection, a Midnight Black DualSense controller is available for sale worldwide.
The only missing piece is the blu-ray disc drive, which is only available in the standard white colour. It's possible Sony is going to bring the Midnight Black version to the rest of the world after it launches in Japan.

63,2026-10-13,"Tamnoon CEO Marina Segal
Cloud security spent a decade learning to see everything. Half of what it found is still open. Tamnoon’s CEO says the next decade belongs to machines that know when not to act, and as of today she is publishing what hers refused.
Marina Segal helped build the machines that see everything.
She was at Dome9 Security when it defined cloud security posture management, and at Check Point after it bought the company. The idea won completely. Today a misconfigured bucket, an open port or an over-permissioned role shows up in a console within minutes of being created, in any cloud, on any of a dozen scanners. Detection is finished. Nobody serious argues otherwise.
Then she watched what happened to the findings.
“Nothing,” she says. “Nothing happened to them. We built an industry that could see every problem in the cloud, and then we filed the problems.”
Her company’s own data puts a figure on the filing cabinet. More than half of everything ever detected in the cloud is still open. A critical finding now takes 150 days on average to heal, and the number is getting worse, not better. By CrowdStrike’s count, an attacker moves from first foothold to lateral movement in 29 minutes. The defender’s clock, once the alert has fired, runs in months.
Segal co-founded Tamnoon in 2021 to close that gap. What she built is stranger than a faster button. She built a machine whose proudest output is a refusal.
The industry’s answer to a remediation backlog was always going to be automation, and in the last eighteen months it arrived in force. Nearly every cloud security platform now ships an agent that offers to make the change itself. The launch posts promise the same things: speed, an approval step, a rollback, control.
Most of those switches stay off.
Ask a cloud security team why, and the answer has nothing to do with the technology. It has to do with the name on the change. In production, every change is somebody’s responsibility, and nobody approves a change a machine cannot explain. A wrong remediation does not look like a security failure. It looks like an outage, with a ticket that says who approved it.
“Every vendor in this market is selling speed,” Segal says. “Speed was never the problem. Nobody was ever afraid the fix would be slow. They were afraid it would be wrong, and that they would be the one who said yes to it.”
She thinks the fear is correct. Not a training problem, not a change-management problem, not a culture problem. A rational response to automation that has never once shown a buyer what it would refuse to do.
“A machine that cannot say no is not automation,” she says. “It’s a liability with an API.”
Here is where Tamnoon parts from the rest of the category, and it parts at the root.
Every remediation product is built to act. Tamnoon’s engine was built to decide, and the first decision it learned was no. Before it was allowed to change anything in a customer’s cloud, it was taught to read: what depends on this resource, who owns it, what changed recently, what the traffic says. Then it was taught to refuse the change it could not prove safe, and to say why.
“A machine learns to act long before it learns when to stop,” Segal says. “We taught ours in the other order. The first thing we automated was restraint. That order is the whole company.”
The demo Tamnoon runs makes the point in under a minute. Six storage buckets, open to the internet, identical in the scanner. Same alert, same severity, same red. The engine reads all six. Three are safe to close, and it closes them, through the customer’s own change process, with a rollback ready. Two it declines, in writing, because the change would have severed something live that the scanner could not see. One it neither closes nor declines. It asks, because the owner is unknown and it will not guess.
One alert. Three correct answers. The scanner was right every time. The scanner was never the problem.
What makes the refusal useful rather than annoying is what comes with it. A decline from the engine carries the finding, what was read first, the verdict, the reason, the one fact that would change the verdict, and the steps to do it by hand. “A refusal without evidence is a shrug,” Segal says. “A refusal with the evidence is a decision you can defend at nine in the morning. Declined is an answer.”
The second break with the category is about trust, and it inverts how the industry has sold automation for twenty years.
The convention is a dial. The vendor ships the capability, the customer chooses how much of it to switch on, and the risk of the choice belongs to the customer. Tamnoon’s engine starts with the dial at zero. It connects read-only and changes nothing in the customer’s cloud. It is granted the right to act on a class of finding only after its record on that class holds, and the customer keeps the veto throughout.
“You keep the veto,” Segal says. “It earns the keys. And it can lose them. Autonomy isn’t a feature you buy. It’s a track record, and a track record is something you can read.”
The unit of that record is the receipt. Every decision the engine makes, closures and refusals alike, becomes one: six fields, readable by an auditor without a translator. When a customer’s engineer overrides the engine, the override goes into the record too, and becomes how the engine judges that environment next time. It gets smarter from the whole fleet and stricter about each customer’s cloud.
“The board will never ask you how fast the fix was,” Segal says. “They will ask how you knew it was safe. We built the answer to the second question.”
Segal is careful with the phrase, because operations people hear it and think of pods restarting.
“Your cloud already restarts itself,” she says. “It has never once closed its own security hole. Healing means the exposure is gone, through your own change process, and someone can defend how.”
Her picture of the next five years is specific. Security teams stop being the liability boundary for changes they did not design. The backlog stops being a filing cabinet and becomes a queue with verdicts in it. Engineers see one problem where they used to see 142 alerts, which is the ratio Tamnoon’s cross-scanner model produces on average. The same eight misconfigurations stop coming back every quarter, because what heals once stays healed. And autonomy stops being a checkbox in a vendor’s settings and becomes something granted per class of finding, like a credit limit, on evidence.
Three predictions come with it, and she is willing to be wrong about them in public. Refusal becomes a line in every remediation RFP. The receipt replaces the score as the thing buyers compare. And no vendor sells remediation without showing a decline.
“In five years nobody will buy remediation from a vendor who can’t show them what it refused,” she says. “The question will sound obvious. Today nobody asks it.”
The customers who have run the engine for a full year describe the change in ratios. One went from 6,074 open findings to 2,041 over four quarters, three times fewer, with 93% fewer new alerts arriving by the end. One healthcare company priced a single finding end to end: $8,775 to close it by hand, $775 through the engine. Warner Music Group is a named customer. Tamnoon has raised more than $18 million, with a Series A led by Bright Pixel Capital, and its engine reads findings from seventeen scanners, Wiz and CrowdStrike Falcon Cloud Security among them.
Today Tamnoon publishes two things this market does not usually publish.
The first is a number. Cloud security has always published how fast attackers move. It has been much quieter about how slowly it heals. The Cloud Remediation Index, out this morning, comes from the same 14.86 million detections behind the company’s report, and it answers two questions in one place: what share of everything ever detected is still open, and how long a critical finding stays open. The attacker’s clock is measured in minutes. This one is measured in months.
The second is a record. As of today, Tamnoon keeps a public page of the remediations its engine declined, each with the six fields.
“Every company in this industry publishes its wins,” Segal says. “We are publishing the changes we did not make. It is a strange list to be proud of, until you ask which of the two lists a security team should read before it lets a machine into production.”
Alongside them, the company has published the six questions it says buyers should put to any remediation vendor, itself included. The first: can it refuse a change, and show one refusal with the evidence attached.
“Everyone demos the fix,” Segal says. “Ask them to demo the decline.”
It is an unusual way to relaunch a security company, leading with what the product will not do and a number the industry rarely publishes about itself. But the conventional way has had a decade, and half of everything it found is still open. Segal’s bet is that the second decade of cloud security belongs to the machine that has earned the right to touch production, one refusal at a time. The company’s own words for it are eight.
Everyone automates the yes. We mastered the no.

67,2026-10-13,"Paul Tudor Jones is warning of the most challenging year the US stock market has seen in decades.
The billionaire hedge funder and the founder of Tudor Investment Corporation is sounding the alarm on the stock market outlook heading into 2027. He sees a variety of headwinds pushing against stock prices next year, potentially resulting in one of the most challenging environments for inflows since the bursting of the dot-com bubble, he said in an interview on CNBC on Tuesday.
Jones noted that the short-term outlook for stocks still looks strong. Historically, in midterm years, the market tends to bottom in October before a strong finish into yearend, he said.
""At the same time, if I was thinking for a year from now, 2027 is going to be really, really challenging time for the stock market,"" Jones said of the medium-term. ""You've probably got the most negative flow picture for the US stock market that we've had, say, since 2002.""
Jones pointed to supply and demand dynamics for US stocks, which has leaned more heavily on the demand side in recent years due to factors like increased corporate buybacks and mergers and acquisitions. 
Going forward, though, stock issuance is expected to surge as more companies go public, insider shares get unlocked for sale, and secondary offerings increase. Altogether, it makes the ""most negative"" picture for inflows the market has seen since 2009, Jones estimated.
Jones also highlighted worrying parallels the market was flashing to 1999, the year the dot-com bubble was enjoying its final hurrah.
The US market now feels ""completely, perfectly bifurcated,"" he said, referring to how the AI sector has thrived and propped up the benchmark indexes, though other sectors of the market have fallen amid the recent surge in bond yields and broader worries about the economy. The same bifurcation in stock performance was also seen prior to the dot-com crash, Jones said.
Around 84% of all companies in the index are trading at least 10% below their all-time highs, meaning the majority of large-cap stocks are in correction territory, according to a recent analysis from The Kobeissi Letter.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
The US also looks to be in a ""two-speed economy,"" Jones added, another parallel to the late 90s. More economists have flagged a K-shaped recovery for the US in recent years, the idea that higher-earning Americans are thriving while lower- to middle-income Americans are struggling to keep pace with the cost-of-living.
""For me, it's just so clear that, again, we're on this path that could be great short-term, but may not be so great long-term,"" Jones said.
""We are literally, exactly like 1999,"" he added of the bifurcation.

68,2026-10-13,"Semrush tracked four AI platforms in the UK and US from January to July. The brands they mention often differ from the sites they cite, and most brands only UK platforms always name are retailers.
ChatGPT leans on news sites more than any other major AI platform, in both the UK and the US, Semrush said on Tuesday. From January to July, news took a bigger share of ChatGPT’s cited sources every month. Gemini, Google’s AI Mode and AI Overviews all trailed it.
The pull is stronger in the UK. News and information sites took 29.5% of citations among ChatGPT’s 100 most-cited UK domains, on average. In the US, the figure was 19.6%.
Semrush, which Adobe owns, sells tools that track how brands appear in AI answers. For the 2026 AI Visibility Index, it ran 2.5 million UK prompts and 25 million US prompts each month. It used ChatGPT, Gemini and Google’s AI Mode. It added Google AI Overviews results for real search keywords. The data covers January to July 2026 and 22 industry categories.
“AI is now a decision layer for customers, changing how they discover brands, sort through options and ultimately purchase,” Andrew Warden, Semrush’s chief marketing officer, said in a statement.
The four platforms also disagree more about which brands to name in the UK. On average, any two of the four platforms shared 63 to 70 of their top 100 mentioned brands in the UK. In the US, the figure was 70 to 78. The UK platforms agreed less on both brands and sources in all seven months measured.
Gemini named more distinct brands per answer than ChatGPT in every month, in both markets. ChatGPT cited the most sources per answer of the four platforms, and Gemini the fewest.
Semrush also found that being mentioned is not the same as being cited as a source. Fewer than half of the top 100 mentioned brands also appeared among the top 100 cited domains, on average across categories and platforms. In the UK, the overlap ranged from 36.7 to 45.3 domains out of 100, depending on the month.
Tesco and Asda show the gap. Both stayed among the top 100 mentioned brands on all four platforms in every month. Yet in July, Tesco was ChatGPT’s 25th most-cited UK domain and Asda its 606th. On Gemini, they ranked 29th and 60th.
Google’s AI Overviews cited medical reference sites more than the other three platforms did, in both markets and every month. In the UK, those sites took 13.4% to 18.9% of its citation weight among leading sources. In the US, they took 8.4% to 13.7%.
Video streaming sites, led by YouTube, took the largest share of AI Overviews citations in both markets.
On Gemini, news led the UK source categories every month. It took 16.6% to 23.0% of top-100 citations.
Publishers’ place in AI answers is already a live issue. Google is paying 100 publishers for AI answers in a pilot, The Information reported last month.
Google’s AI Mode also leaned on a small group of sites. In the UK, its ten leading source domains took 23.2% to 35.8% of citations among its 10,000 most-cited domains. For ChatGPT, the figure was 12.8% to 15.4%.
Semrush counted 24 UK domains and 36 US domains that made the top 100 mentions on all four platforms every month. It calls this group the Universal Core. Fifteen domains are shared, including Adobe, Apple, Amazon, Google, Microsoft, Reddit, TikTok and YouTube.
Six of the nine domains found only in the UK group are retailers: Amazon UK, Argos, Asda, eBay UK, Marks and Spencer and Tesco. The BBC, Nike and TripAdvisor UK complete it. The US-only group includes carmakers, sports leagues, entertainment brands and retailers.
The report also cites Adobe data showing that visitors referred by AI convert 60% better than other traffic and bring 53% more revenue per visit.
Semrush is one of several companies selling brand visibility in AI answers. Profound raised $180m at a $1.8bn valuation in September. Semrush itself launched a brand visibility framework in April.

The UK needs to change these things

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Consultancy firm Kearney has branded the UK as one of Europe's slowest-growing data center markets, despite the government's positive narrative, and it could all be down to regulation.
At present, the UK is said to be Europe's second-largest data center market, however demand is only expected to grow by 17.6% annually between now and 2030, putting it second-last only to Germany (16.2%).
On the flip side, Ireland is slated to see an annual growth rate of 36% – around double the UK's.
Kearney doesn't blame a lack of demand for the slower-than-average growth, but rather its poor ability to accommodate new infrastructure efficiently and affordably.
The consultancy firm sees energy availability as a major blocker across Europe as a whole, with forecasts of 236 Two by 2035 just for data center consumption – up from 96 TWh in 2024.
But beyond energy constraints, which are pretty widespread, the research also reveals that high land prices could be making UK data centers less competitive.
Typical costs for British and other Western European sites are around €2.25 million per megawatt, but Nordic markets are able to hit a low average of €600,000 per megawatt. It puts Finland and Norway's anticipated annual demand growth at 37.6% and 36.8% respectively between now and 2030.
Sign up to the TechRadar Pro newsletter to get all the top news, opinion, features and guidance your business needs to succeed!
""What has changed is that power and land are scarce enough that the next wave of capacity must match the workloads that genuinely need to be close to users, customers, and national jurisdictions,"" European Data Center Lead Christoph Neunkirchen explained. ""Everything else will go where it is easier to build.""
For the UK in particular, whose government says at least 6GW of AI data center capacity will be needed by 2030, Kearney emphasises the need for lower electricity prices, cheaper land and faster grid connections.

72,2026-10-13," The Seattle-based visual-content provider expects to complete the restructuring by year-end [1]. Getty family members and Koch Equity Development affiliates will provide the equity investment as part of the restructuring support agreement, according to the report [2][1].Debt Restructuring Terms and Creditor Support Holders representing 83.5% of Getty's unsecured bonds have agreed to retire $270 million of outstanding senior unsecured notes, reducing the company's debt burden, according to the report. Investors holding about 89% of secured bonds and term loans, along with all revolving credit facility lenders, have agreed to lower interest obligations and extend secured debt maturities [1]. The deal includes $143 million in financing to keep Getty afloat, the report stated. No direct quotes from company officials or creditors appear in the source material; information is attributed to Bloomberg and the reported agreement [1].AI Disruption in Stock Media Market Getty's licensing business serves news outlets, advertisers and businesses, but AI-generated images and video could replace stock-content purchases, according to the report. Chatbots allow users to create visual content at lower cost, pressuring licensing prices across the industry. Shutterstock has likely felt similar pain from AI-generated content, and Adobe Stock also faces substitution risk [3][1]. The report noted that the company plans to begin an expedited prepackaged Chapter 11 process [4]. The adoption of AI chatbots for professional and personal use has grown since late 2022, coinciding with Getty's share decline. Market observers cited by Bloomberg indicate that AI tools are changing how businesses acquire visual media [1].Industry and Market Reactions Getty's shares have fallen sharply since ChatGPT's launch, reflecting investor concerns about AI competition. Benzinga reported that Getty's unsecured bonds trade at 47 cents and the stock is down 93% in a year [5]. The restructuring aims to keep the company operating while it adjusts to the shifting stock-content market. Creditors holding most of Getty's bonds and loans have agreed to the terms, according to Bloomberg [1]. Questions remain about the long-term viability of traditional stock-photo licensing as AI tools improve. The report did not include independent confirmation from Getty or its creditors beyond the described support agreement [1].Outlook for Getty and Stock Content Sector Getty expects to complete the Chapter 11 restructuring by year-end, according to the report. The $533 million equity commitment and $143 million financing are intended to provide liquidity during the process [1]. The company seeks to reduce its debt load and extend secured debt maturities. 73,2026-10-13,"Tokenized equities have reached nearly $3.3 billion in value as investors embrace round-the-clock access beyond Wall Street hours.


Most trading in tokenized stocks on major blockchains now takes place outside regular US market hours, offering an early indication that around-the-clock trading could be one of the biggest advantages of putting equities onchain.
According to Blockworks data cited by The Kobeissi Letter, more than 70% of tokenized stock trading volume on BNB Chain occurs outside the traditional 9:30 am to 4 pm Eastern trading session. The figure is 63% on Solana and nearly 60% on Base.
Meanwhile, Jupiter, a major Solana-based trading platform, reported a 50% month-over-month increase in tokenized asset trading volume.

Source: The Kobeissi Letter
The figures suggest investors use tokenized stocks to trade when traditional US exchanges are closed, rather than simply replicating activity during regular market hours. 
The growth comes as tokenized stock values have increased by 15% over the past 30 days to nearly $3.3 billion, according to RWA.xyz data. A significant portion of that value is concentrated among issuers such as Ondo Global Markets, xStocks and bStocks, whose tokens are issued and traded across different blockchains.
Related: Winners and losers of the SEC’s new tokenized stocks rules
The growth in round-the-clock trading comes as US regulators begin opening the door to tokenized equities in traditional markets. Last month, the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) introduced an “innovation exemption” allowing qualifying venues to trade tokenized US-listed stocks on public blockchains.
SEC Commissioner Mark Uyeda said participating venues must publish transaction data in US dollars, including trade prices, sizes, timestamps and daily trading volumes. They must also disclose liquidity pool addresses and balances.
Unlike existing tokenized stock products such as xStocks and Ondo Global Markets, which are generally unavailable to US investors, the SEC exemption allows qualifying venues to offer tokenized US equities domestically, provided they preserve shareholder rights and meet trading and disclosure requirements.
Related: Crypto Biz: Wealthy investors are buying crypto, but their advisers aren’t sold

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy."

EXCLUSIVE: The Black List has named the six writers and projects chosen for its 2026 Writers Lab. They are Elton de Almeida (A Mark of Kin), Yasmin DeWinter (Guillaume), Scott Leddy (Nightwatch), Marcelle Pallais (One Last Summer), Alexander Seltzer and Kyle Jutkiewicz (Depths) and Emma Taylor Stock (Denial).

	The program, now in its 13th year, provides creative mentorship and professional support to promising feature writers as they further develop their selected project during a weeklong workshop in Ojai, CA.

	The Black List’s Annual Lab program supports two distinct cohorts: writers and ﬁlmmakers. The Writers Lab focuses on developing feature scripts to be sold in the studio system and/or used as voice samples. The Projects Lab, which will be announced in November, offers writer-directors the chance to develop a screenplay that they intend to direct.

	During the Lab, each writer will develop their screenplay through peer workshops and one-on-one sessions with working professional screenwriters, including returning mentors Travis Braun (One Night Only), Stephany Folsom (Toy Story 4, The Lord of the Rings: Rings of Power), Katie Silberman (Set It Up, Don’t Worry Darling), Victoria Strouse (Seekers of Infinite Love, Finding Dory) and Christina Hodson (Birds of Prey, Bumblebee).

	Black List Annual Lab alumna Savion Einstein (A Cuban Girl’s Guide To Tea and Tomorrow) will also join as a session mentor. After the lab concludes, the writers cohort will participate in a yearlong support program involving monthly meetings with screenwriters, agents, managers, producers, and executives. Pitching and peer workshop sessions will be led by Scott Myers (The Protagonist’s Journey, Spec Script Deals: 1991-2025) who has been a part of every single annual Black List Lab so far.

	“We’re excited to welcome the latest cohort of screenwriters to the Black List Lab fold. At a time when the industry can seem particularly challenging and harder than ever to break into, we’re thrilled to be able to support projects that make us excited to go to the movies. These writers are bringing distinctive voices, bold points of view, and stories that deserve to be heard to the Lab and we’re privileged to be able to give them space to take risks, challenge themselves, and build community with other writers, in Ojai and beyond,” said Megan Halpern, Black List SVP.

75,2026-10-13,"TOPICS

                    security
                

        Stock spoiled at hundreds of stores as manufacturer says affected equipment ran outdated firmware
    
Cyberattacks on supermarket refrigeration systems reportedly pushed temperatures as high as 50°C (122°F), affecting hundreds of stores in France and forcing retailers to discard food.
Major supermarkets such as Carrefour, Coopérative U, Intermarché, Monoprix, and more were hit by the attacks, which spoiled perishable food and left stores without fresh produce.
The affected equipment includes cold-storage units used to hold food before it reaches supermarket shelves. The criminals behind the attack raised temperatures to unsafe levels, spoiling food and in some cases starting fires.
Some affected retailers used refrigeration controllers produced by Danish company Danfoss.
Authorities have not identified the entry point or confirmed exploitation of the vulnerabilities previously disclosed by Claroty and patched in August 2025, although the manufacturer said affected stores were running outdated firmware.
Experts say the previously identified vulnerabilities were easily scriptable and relied on a unique but predictable authentication method.
Claroty found a hidden authentication mechanism in the AK-SM 800A platform that accepted a code generated from the device's current date, bypassing the configured administrator password.
Anyone who reproduced the algorithm could generate a valid daily code and gain administrative access.
""It's a zero-skill thing to pull off, I wrote the bash script while having a shit,"" said security pro Kevin Beaumont.
The research demonstrated how compromised controllers could expose refrigeration systems to physical disruption, although the vulnerabilities' role in this week's attacks remains unconfirmed.
Danfoss told reporters this week that the French supermarkets affected by the attacks ""were running outdated firmware"" and that the company has consistently advocated for keeping systems updated.
The Register asked Danfoss if it would confirm whether the previously disclosed bugs were exploited to execute the attacks, and whether the same bug was also abused in August to compromise US military commissary refrigerators, which led to food losses.
CERT-FR published an alert reporting numerous compromises of Danfoss AK-SM and AK-SC controllers, which were not mentioned in Claroty's original reporting nor in Danfoss's advisories at the time.
The authority advised disconnecting or restricting network access. For suspected compromises, it recommended a factory reset, security patches, credential rotation, account checks, and full reconfiguration.
For those wondering why fridges even need to be reachable via the internet, the main reason is that it allows central engineering teams to troubleshoot issues remotely, according to Claroty's researchers.
""Industrial control systems such as refrigeration controllers are often assumed to be on safely isolated networks. However, our research found many of these devices are directly exposed to the public internet,"" said Amir Preminger, CTO at Claroty. ""While this convenience significantly reduces maintenance costs by allowing engineers to troubleshoot issues remotely, it also lowers the barrier to entry for attackers.
""Organizations must take proactive measures to ensure that management interfaces are not insecurely connected to the internet, and ensure that access to administrative services is restricted to trusted management networks or secured through VPNs and other appropriate network segmentation controls.""
Intermarché owner Groupement Les Mousquetaires said around 150 of its supermarkets were affected by the attacks, while Carrefour confirmed around 80 impacted stores.
Monoprix said roughly 5 percent of its 490 stores were experiencing the same issues, and Coopérative U CEO Dominique Schelcher said 80 of the company’s 2,000 stores were hit, adding that the attack on the food sector was “unacceptable.”
“Targeting equipment essential to the preservation of fresh products is an attack on the French food supply chain,” he said. “It is unprecedented, extremely serious, and unacceptable. We remain fully mobilized.”
However, in a separate twist, Schelcher told French radio station RTL on Tuesday that Coopérative U is not a customer of Danfoss.
The Register contacted Coopérative U for more information about what caused the attacks at its stores.
Shodan searches for internet-facing Danfoss controller interfaces indicated that more than 4,500 endpoints were exposed as of Monday, with more than half of those located in France.
Ahold Delhaize, a major grocery retailer in the Netherlands, announced on the same day that it was experiencing a ""security issue."" No link to the refrigeration attacks in France has been established.
The end result is similar, however, as the company's grocery brands are reporting stock issues.
Several of the group's brands have taken their websites offline and posted notices about the ""cybersecurity incident.""
Stores remain open, but affected brands have reported disruption to websites, apps, customer services, and online deliveries.
The disruption hit the company's distribution systems, although these are now being restored.
Updates posted to social media on Tuesday told shoppers that staff were once again able to use digital shelf labels but stock levels may vary across stores, and long-life items were likely to be more abundant than fresh fruit and vegetables.
The retailer previously suffered a cyberattack in November 2024 that disrupted its US operations. ®
