78,2026-10-13,"Sheikh Mansour bin Zayed Al Nahyan (centre) bought Man City in a Â£200m deal in 2008
Manchester City Council has made just under Â£3m from a deal to share in the so-called ""super-profits"" from developments built on its land by Manchester City's owners.
A deal struck in 2014 meant the council would receive payments if the profits made by Abu Dhabi United Group (ADUG) on schemes in Ancoats and New Islington exceeded a certain level.
But Prof Adam Leaver, co-author of a 2022 report by the University of Sheffield into the council's relationship with ADUG, said commercial secrecy around the arrangements meant it was hard to know if the council got the best value it could.
The council said it expected another payment soon, and that the regeneration had been worth Â£1.25bn to the city.
In 2008, Manchester City was bought for Â£200m by ADUG, owned by Sheikh Mansour bin Zayed Al Nahyan - a senior member of the Abu Dhabi ruling family, who is a current vice president and deputy prime minister of the United Arab Emirates (UAE).
In the next decade, the club went on to win the first three of eight Premier League titles, and ADUG and Manchester City Council became partners in a company called Manchester Life.
But in 2018, the Premier League began investigating the club's financial dealings, and, last week, it announced the club had been found guilty of 115 charges, including some around a so-called disguised funding scheme, which was said to have seen the club's owners topping up sponsorship and commercial deals with more than Â£800m of their own funds.
The club denies wrongdoing and has lodged an appeal against the guilty verdict. 
Since then, Manchester City Council's relationship with ADUG has come under renewed scrutiny.
Adam Leaver was a co-author of a report looking at the deal between the council and ADUG 
Under the terms of the Manchester Life partnership, ADUG was able to lease development sites owned by the council across east Manchester, near to the club's stadium.
The council and ADUG struck an overage deal. Such deals are common in partnerships between public authorities and private developers, and normally ensure that if the value of a plot of land increases beyond a certain limit because of development or it being sold, the authority receives a share of what are sometimes known as the ""super-profits"".
According to Leaver's 2022 Manchester Offshored report, about 10 acres of land were transferred into Manchester Life's control.
The report estimated about Â£112m of property sales had been made by companies ultimatley owned in Abu Dhabi. 
Leaver told the BBC that it was hard to gauge how good a deal the council had struck.
ADUG went into partnership with Manchester City Council to develop land around east Manchester
He said it was not possible to see how much profit had been remitted to ADUG from connected companies registered in Jersey, where there is far less transparency about companies' finances.
""So we can't compare how much profit was received by the council relative to their ADUG partner,"" he said.
He added the fact that the overage agreement's terms were concealed from public view meant it was not possible to know how much profit ADUG needed to make before it was obligated to give a share to the council.
In July this year, the council revealed that reviews of how it managed and monitored overage deals with developers found ""weaknesses in documentation, monitoring and assurance, particularly around historic overage arrangements"".
""It's unclear what prompted this concern with overage, but it doesn't look great,"" Leaver said.
The council said the deals had been made in the wake of the credit crunch and the financial crash of 2008, when there was ""very little market interest in the area"".
A spokesman added that ""each parcel of land was valued by independent expertsâ¦ and the best overall deal was achieved for each site"".
But Leaver said while developments in Manchester had ""dwindled until around 2012,"" the market had ""picked up from 2013 onwards"", around the time the Manchester Life partnership was formed.
He pointed to what he called ""significant investment"" by 2014 and 2015, and a ""growing new population in Ancoats"", the Royal Mills project, the Ice Plant development, and the regeneration of Cutting Room Square.
""Small trendy businesses were also in the process of moving into the area, indicating that gentrification was already under way in Ancoats as a natural spillover from the successful regeneration of the Northern Quarter, before the Manchester Life agreement was sealed,"" Leaver said.
""Land values were rising and developers were beginning to rediscover profitable opportunities and take more risk following the financial crisis,"" he added.
The original Manchester Life plan envisioned more than 6,000 homes built with investment of up to Â£1bn over a decade.
So far, the schemes have delivered about 1,500 homes, across nine sites in a ""Â£1.25bn programme of regeneration,"" according to the council.
It is not clear whether the difference in the outcome and the original plan has had any impact on how much the council was in line to receive from the overage deal.
But the council said it had calculated that the overall payment to the council equated to about ""Â£26,000 per homeâ¦ representing an above-market return for land that had failed to attract market interest since the 2008 financial crisis"".
The spokesman added: ""This includes 1,500 new homes - with hundreds more on site now - the preservation of two historic mills, 30,000 sq ft of commercial space attracting a wave of independent businesses, and thousands of new residents, while also generating above market land returns for the council.
""The regeneration of Ancoats and New Islington isâ¦ an internationally recognised success story - not least Ancoats being named one of the best places to live globally.
""More than 3,400 homes have since been completed in the area by a range of developers, including homes for social rent and affordable tenures"".
Sir Richard Leese said the Abu Dhabi deals had been great for Manchester
Sir Richard Leese, who was leader of the city council when the deals were struck and is now honorary president of Man City, has told the BBC ""the investment from Abu Dhabi is what created the value"".
But Leaver said his ""own conversation suggest that was not the case, and that there was quite a bit of interest in developing [the] land"".
The fallout from the Premier League investigation has prompted concern that wider UAE investment in UK projects could suffer, with media outlet Bloomberg reporting that Emirates officials had warned it ""could have an influence on the bilateral state relationship"".
Before that warning came, Prime Minister Andy Burnham â who was metropolitan mayor of Manchester from 2017 until entering Downing Street this summer â said he would be ""really concerned"" if ADUG was to pull out of Manchester over the Premier League investigation.
Burnham's intervention prompted criticism from political opponents. Shadow sports minister Louie French was reported to have accused Burnham of ""publicly backing the owners"".
Burnham has now said he will publish details of all his dealings with Man City and its owners ADUG during his time in charge in Manchester.
""I've been asked by the leader of the opposition to publish details of my meetings, which I'm happy to do and I'm going to,"" he said.
""I will quite clearly put out there everything that I have done working with them in my previous role as Mayor of Greater Manchester.""

79,2026-10-13,"Happy Tuesday! It's October 13, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.
In this morning's edition we're looking at Dodge's desire for a new V8 Hellcat, as well as Stellantis' refusal to offload brands. We'll also look at auto sales in China, how BMW is doing badly, and Volkswagen is doing even worse. 
If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.
Stellantis' marketing strategy with its American brands over the past few years has been to lean into being As Chud As Possible — look no further than the whole ""Symbol of Protest"" Hemi badging for proof. The electric Charger Daytona was an unfortunate misstep in that plan, being too woke (electric) for traditional Hemi buyers yet too unwoke (enormous dumb-sounding coupe) to capture new customers. Stellantis made a half-measure towards rectifying that already with the Hurricane six-cylinder, but now it seems the Charger is returning to its roots: A Hemi under the hood. From Automotive News: 
Stellantis is planning a V-8 engine variant of the Dodge Charger as the automaker leans harder on the Hemi to win back customers.
The company aims to launch production of the 6.2-liter Hellcat muscle car at the end of 2027 for the 2028 model year, according to a planning document reviewed by Automotive News. The company has informed suppliers that it intends to build around 12,000 of them per year.
The supercharged Hemi V-8 has been rumored for months, but Stellantis has not confirmed it. A Stellantis spokesperson told Automotive News that the launch details are ""purely speculation/rumor"" and that the company ""will not comment on future product strategy.""
Electric power is faster than internal combustion, without question — torque available from zero RPM is tough to beat. So Dodge's goal here is to actually decrease the performance of its flagship performance car, make it slower, in order to appease buyers who fear new things. The car will remain, inherently, new. It's a bold strategy, Cotton. 
While Dodge, Jeep, and Ram have dialed in their American marketing, the brands still have to play under the same roof as international marques like Fiat, Vauxhall, and Peugeot. Those latter brands need to sell in Europe and Asia, where the same ""return to tradition"" messaging doesn't quite hit, which has led some folks to ask whether Stellantis should split things up. Antonio Filosa, for his part, doesn't think so. From Automotive News: 
Stellantis CEO Antonio Filosa rejected the notion of separating the group's European and U.S. brands, saying the company's strategy is based on all 14 of them ""playing their unique role.""
""It is not plausible at all,"" Filosa told Bloomberg Television Oct. 12 at the Paris auto show when asked about the idea of separating brands such as Citroen and Fiat from Jeep and Ram. Stellantis develops technologies globally for all its models, including powertrains and computing architecture, and each brand will use that as a base, he added.
Filosa has been reviewing the group's strategy as part of an effort to better allocate spending among its brands, many of which cater to the same mass-market customers.
Marketing helps, but there's still a core issue where environmentally conscious fuel efficient Fiats have to ride on the same platform as screw-the-haters, middle-finger-to-the-sky Jeeps. I wouldn't want to be the engineer who has to design one platform for both needs. 
Chinese customers are buying fewer cars, after years spent rapidly grabbing the latest and greatest on the automotive market, and the cars they're still buying are largely domestic. Import brands are feeling the pinch, and BMW has the numbers to prove it. From the Wall Street Journal: 
BMW group vehicle deliveries fell in the third quarter as the continuing challenging market in China offset growth in Europe and the U.S.
The German automaker, which houses its namesake brand as well as the Mini and Rolls-Royce marques and a motorcycle business, said Tuesday that overall group sales fell 6.3% in the quarter to 551,072 vehicles.
...
Sales of BMW and Mini branded cars rose 2.7% in Europe and 2.5% in the U.S., but fell by 25% in China to 114,355 vehicles, with BMW noting the figures reflect the performance of the automotive market as a whole.
The automotive market is certainly down, as BMW says, but 25% is a big gap to try and make up. If you're wondering why every automaker under the sun is firing every worker it can find, look no further than lost profits from China. 
What's worse than a 25% drop in sales, though? A 39% drop in sales! That's right, BMW's numbers are bad, but Volkswagen's are even worse. From Automotive News: 
Volkswagen Group reported a 14 percent decline in global deliveries in the third quarter Oct. 12, its sharpest drop in more than four years, as weakness in China, its largest market, continued to weigh on sales.
Deliveries, a closely watched proxy for sales, fell to 1.89 million vehicles in the quarter.
Deliveries in China tumbled 39 percent in the quarter and were down 30 percent in the first nine months of the year. Deliveries fell 5.8 percent in North America and 2.8 percent in Western Europe but rose 1.4 percent in Central and Eastern Europe.
Western automakers love the idea of an isolationist market at home, but hate one abroad — Europe shouldn't allow Chinese cars in, but China and India should buy as many European vehicles as the companies can possibly ship over. This is a rational and normal way to look at international business, and a good way to look at future plans. 


Why do we keep putting our leaders in isolated mansions? The President should have to live in a three-bedroom apartment in DC with two roommates that don't do the dishes. Give our leaders a real authentic American experience. 
It looks like this is just where prices are going to sit until we get some new updates with the Strait of Hormuz. Have fun with that!
Sometimes that's just the way of it. "

					By Melanie Goodfellow 
Senior International Film Correspondent

EXCLUSIVE: Director and cinematographer Alexander Witt is set to direct Feathering the Wind, a coming-of-age feature set against the high-speed world of competitive youth sailboat racing as its producers gear up to take the production to the market at the AFM.

	Witt has built an extensive career directing and shooting second-unit action for some of Hollywood’s biggest films and franchises, with credits including Gladiator, The Bourne Identity, the James Bond films Skyfall and Casino Royale, Marvel Studios’ Avengers: Infinity War and Michael Mann’s Ferrari.

	He also brings decades of experience directing commercials and features. His feature directing credits include Resident Evil: Apocalypse and the Sayen film trilogy. His commercial work includes campaigns for Reebok, Honda and Helly Hansen, among others.

	Actor-producer-writer Christopher Atkins and Michelle Alexandria of Light Year Pictures are producing. Atkins, who first broke onto the big screen opposite Brooke Shields in The Blue Lagoon has more than 80 acting credits to his name.  Alexandria is executive producer on the upcoming Maserati: The Brothers with other productions in the works including Kaitlyn’s Chance and Ethan Almighty.

Feathering the Wind is billed as a timeless coming-of-age story set against the speed, danger and intensity of competitive youth sailboat racing.

	“I’m really excited to have Alexander on board,” said Atkins. “It’s a timeless coming of age wrapped in incredible high-speed youth sailing sequences – an F1 on the water. Alexander brings sensitivity to the story and the action experience to bring it to the screen. It’s a perfect match.”

	“Alexander’s extraordinary body of work brings a level of skill, vision and prestige that is invaluable. The beauty, speed and intensity of sailing deserve to be experienced on the big screen, and I can’t wait to see Alexander make this film his own,” said Alexandria.

	Also attached to the filmmaking team is veteran aerial and aquatic cinematographer Jordan “Jordy” Klein Jr., whose credits include The Abyss, Cocoon and Splash. Klein began his career working alongside his father, Academy Award-winning underwater cinematographer Jordan Klein Sr., building a career that has spanned decades of aquatic and aerial filmmaking.

	










Feathering the Wind is produced by Light Year Pictures and is represented by entertainment attorney Peter Capozzi of Kelley Martin Capozzi LLP. Additional casting and production details will be announced in the coming weeks.

81,2026-10-13,"Recent market data from IDC Global and Omdia reveals that global computer sales plunged by more than 20 percent year-over-year in the third quarter of 2026. This historic contraction is a direct result of major technology corporations heavily prioritizing artificial intelligence data centers, triggering severe memory shortages that have severely impacted the consumer electronics market. 
 
The rapid expansion of AI infrastructure redirected vital manufacturing resources toward High Bandwidth Memory (HBM) essential for AI graphics processing units. Consequently, component supplies for the broader consumer market dried up. The crunch intensified when key manufacturers, such as Micron, discontinued mainstream consumer brands like Crucial. With empty shelves across retail channels, component costs skyrocketed; high-end 128 GB DDR5 memory kits surged by as much as 500 percent, reaching up to 3,399 US dollars. 
 
Major PC vendors took the hardest hit from this supply chain shock. HP experienced the steepest decline with a 30.9 percent drop, followed closely by Dell at 25 percent and Lenovo at 22.6 percent. Brands with specialized ecosystems faced smaller contractions, with Apple down 11.3 percent and ASUS down 8.6 percent. Despite these losses, the top three market leaders maintained their rankings, led by Lenovo with a 23.8 percent market share, HP at 16.5 percent, and Dell at 12.1 percent. 
Global #PC shipments fell 21.2% YoY in 3Q26 as rising component costs and subsiding demand impacted the market, according to new Omdia research.  Read the full press release here: https://t.co/YVZJ0ypD3f pic.twitter.com/QXGoxLmDIL
— Omdia (@OmdiaHQ) October 9, 2026

Industry experts hold divided expectations for the future. While Acer CEO Jason Chen suggests that supply panic is exaggerated and largely serves to protect manufacturer profit margins, major DRAM suppliers indicate that high prices will persist due to rising demand for physical AI infrastructure and robotics. Analysts from IDC caution that while high retail prices may suppress demand and trigger short-term promotions, hardware costs will remain elevated compared to previous years, with further market contractions anticipated heading into 2027. 
Filed in Computers. Read more about  AI (Artificial Intelligence), Idc, Market and RAMmageddon. "

84,2026-10-13,"We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.
Mini PCs have come a long way from their roots as slow, finicky office machines. Computer technology has advanced significantly over the last decade, and stronger hardware keeps getting crammed into smaller spaces. You'd be shocked at the performance you can squeeze out of a device that fits in the palm of your hand.
We went through the field of mini PCs and ruled out anything that didn't have the specs or reviews to prove it could rival a modern desktop tower PC in terms of performance. That still left quite a few mini PCs to choose from at several price points, so we selected the finalists you'll see below based on their strengths and the use cases they fill. Ultimately, you may not need to blow thousands of dollars if you just need a small PC with a lot of oomph. For instance, the reliable Mac mini is still a great value (compared to other expensive mini computers), and there are Windows alternatives now like Lenovo's Yoga Mini.
For gamers, there are more expensive options like the Asus ROG NUC 16, easily one of the most powerful mini PCs on the market. If you're scared off by its $2,500 price tag, there are cheaper alternatives like the Minisforum AtomMan. The mini PC space isn't just limited to the devices you can find on store shelves, either — there are high-end custom options from companies like Framework.
No matter what you need a mini PC for, the Asus ROG NUC 16 can handle it. Starting at a staggering $2,499, the ROG NUC 16 crams a ridiculous amount of power into a relatively tiny form factor. Even with upgrades, the ROG NUC 16 measures roughly 11 inches tall, 7 inches wide, and 2 inches thick.
The basic model includes an Intel Core Ultra 7 270HX Plus processor and an Nvidia RTX 5060 laptop graphics card. The more expensive models all feature an upgraded Intel Core Ultra 9 290HX Plus and either an RTX 5070Ti or RTX 5080 laptop GPU. It can easily handle AAA games like ""Cyberpunk 2077,"" matching even the most expensive desktops like the HP Omen 45L and MSI Aegis RS2.
All of this power is packed into a chassis that weighs just under 7 pounds. The form factor doesn't sacrifice connectivity, either, as the ROG NUC 16 includes multiple USB 3.0 ports, two DisplayPort 2.1 ports, two full-sized HDMI ports, a 2.5 Gbps LAN port, and more. The device can even be positioned both horizontally and vertically, with a special ""G-sensor"" detecting its orientation and adjusting cooling accordingly.
The Mac mini is one of the most-recommended mini PCs out there, and for good reason. Apple has spent two decades perfecting the cooling, performance, and form factor of the Mac mini. Windows versus macOS is still a hot debate, but the Mac mini can handle nearly everything a small Windows PC can. Plus, the 5-by-5-inch form factor is incredibly enticing.
There are multiple Mac mini models to choose from, but the best options are the two newest. The newly released Mac mini M6 comes in at $899, but the slightly older Mac mini M5 Pro gets you even more power with a heftier $1,699 price tag to match. The M6 model is perfect for web-based work and light video editing, but dedicated creators with heavier workloads should spring for the M5 Pro.
Going with a Mac mini does come with some drawbacks, however. You won't get the same range of connections as on some other mini PCs since the Mac mini lacks USB-A and full-size DisplayPort ports, leaving you dependent on adapters, a hub, or replacement cables for peripherals that use them. Hardcore gaming is largely out of the question as well, as most games lack native macOS support — although there are technically ways to play popular PC games on Mac.
If you're dead set on a Windows machine but love the elegance of the Mac mini, the Lenovo Yoga Mini is the device for you. It's clear that Lenovo was essentially trying to make a Windows version of a Mac mini, and the Yoga Mini is a very close match.
The Yoga Mini is ultra-compact with a nearly identical form factor to the Mac mini. It's actually a tad flatter than the Mac mini at 1.91 inches thick, but it's a little bit wider at 5.12 inches. It comes with a Thunderbolt 4 port, two USB-C ports (one with DisplayPort, another with power delivery), an HDMI 2.1 port, and a 2.5 Gbps Ethernet port on the back — plus customizable RGB under-glow on the bottom.
The cheapest configuration is priced at $999, while the most expensive model has a beefy Intel Core Ultra X7 358H processor for $1,869. Both configurations have impressive gaming and productivity performance, and the higher-end version can even match the performance of much larger desktops like the HP Omen 16L, running games like ""Arc Raiders"" flawlessly.
The Minisforum AtomMan is like a slightly larger version of the Asus ROG NUC 16, but without all the gamer flair. Measuring 8-by-12-by-2 inches, the AtomMan takes advantage of the roomier chassis to pack in fully featured desktop hardware. It's the same stuff you'd see inside the best desktop PCs, just packed into a tiny case.
The cheaper AtomMan G7 Pro model is priced at $1,699 and comes equipped with an Intel i9 14900HX and Nvidia RTX 5070 laptop GPU. The more expensive G1 Pro improves its specs at $2,149 with an AMD Ryzen 9 8945HX processor and Nvidia RTX 5060 desktop graphics card. The 5060 might seem like a downgrade at first glance, but it's a full dedicated desktop graphics card, not a laptop version.
The G1 Pro can handle AAA games at 1440p resolution without breaking a sweat, and you can achieve some impressive frame rates if you're only gaming at 1080p. That desktop RTX 5060 is the exact same one you can find in other prebuilt PCs at this price point like the Corsair Vengeance A7500, and the included Ryzen 9 processor can easily handle the latest games.
If none of the prebuilt options catch your eye, then you can spring for a custom configuration from a company like Framework. The price range on Framework PCs varies significantly since you can choose the parts yourself, and the top-end configurations can outperform some of the strongest desktop towers.
The Framework Desktop is similar in size to a Steam Machine, measuring roughly 4-by-8-by-9 inches. Prices start at $1,269 and reach as high as $3,449. The basic $1,269 configuration gets you an AMD AI Max 385 processor, but you'll have to spend at least $1,959 to get the more powerful AMD AI Max 395. 
AMD's AI Max processors are still relatively new, but they boast impressive gaming performance. At 1440p with ultra graphics settings, the AI Max 385 and 395 run ""Monster Hunter Wilds"" at averages of 67 FPS and 85 FPS, respectively. With ""Cyberpunk 2077"" at high settings, both processors average roughly 70 FPS. Both processors are also great for productivity, with Framework even letting you add a ton of memory to your configuration to run large language models.
Our main consideration when making this list was to find mini PCs that feature enough raw power to serve as legit alternatives to desktop towers, rather than machines that were only suitable for basic browsing and office work. We focused on models with capable processors and graphics hardware, looking through their spec sheets and considering expert reviews to assess how they handle demanding tasks like gaming, video editing, and other productivity work. Size also mattered, since the appeal of these machines is getting that level of performance without giving up a large portion of your desk.
For the final five mini PCs you see above, we wanted to offer a selection that spanned different use cases and budgets. That's why you'll see gaming-focused Windows machines alongside a Mac and a fully customizable option. The prices range from $899 for the cheapest option (the base Mac mini M6) to $3,499 (the highest configuration Framework model), but most of the models mentioned fall in a price range of roughly $1,000 to $2,500 — in other words, there should be something on this list for just about anyone."

86,2026-10-13,"Traders betting on SanDisk (SNDK) lost more to forced liquidations over the past 24 hours than traders betting on XRP — a sign of how much leverage on crypto exchanges now sits on chip stocks.
Over $800 million in leveraged positions were liquidated across 10 exchanges in the past 24 hours as bitcoin BTC$83,498.63 dropped to just under $81,000 in early Asian hours.
Most of the damage came in a burst. About $595 million, nearly three-quarters of the day’s total, was liquidated in the last 12 hours, as South Korea’s KOSPI fell more than 6% and bitcoin slid to just under $81,000. 
The largest single wipeout was a $9.65 million bitcoin long on Binance, closed at about $81,700, with the exchange accounting for half of all liquidations.
Read More: Bitcoin rebounds above $83,000 as it decouples from tumbling Korean stocks and Nasdaq futures
A liquidation happens when an exchange forcibly closes a trader’s borrowed position because their losses have eaten through the collateral. Longs, or bets on higher prices, accounted for $694 million of that, or 86% of the total.
Bitcoin led with $181 million in liquidations, and ether was close behind at $178 million despite its much smaller market.
Stock contracts showed up next. Perpetual futures tracking SanDisk, the U.S. memory-chip maker, saw $19.5 million liquidated, just above HYPE's $19 million and XRP's $18.78 million. These perpetual futures let traders bet on a stock’s price around the clock on crypto venues, without an expiry date.
Contracts on SK Hynix and Micron, along with SOXL, a triple-leveraged U.S. semiconductor ETF, also appeared on the heatmap, with $7.21 million liquidated on SOXL.
Those losses lined up with the selloff in Seoul. Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix each fell about 8% on worries over AI-related valuations and competition from Chinese memory-chip makers, as CoinDesk reported.
Oil went the other way. Contracts on WTI and Brent crude were among the few on the map where short bets made up most of the liquidations, as Brent held near $106 after a three-day rally.
Meanwhile, the flow has since reversed. Of the $74 million liquidated over the past four hours, $59 million, or 79%, came from shorts, as bitcoin climbed back above $83,000.
With bullish bets behind 86% of the day’s wipeouts and short sellers now taking the losses, bitcoin heads into Thursday’s U.S. inflation report with much of its long leverage cleared out.

Reels and short-form videos might dominate social feeds right now, but nothing beats a classic image meme. With just a few simple words — or sometimes none at all — a single meme can deliver a quick laugh in less than two seconds.

A basic stock photo can perfectly sum up the struggle of maintaining workplace relationships. A classic historical painting can be repurposed to feel like that chaotic photo your friend snapped of you on a wild night out.
We collected some of the funniest memes from a popular online page that continuously updates its collection with pure internet humor. You might find a few painfully relatable, but we guarantee most will give you a well-deserved laugh.

Not to rain on the parade, but it is no secret that the world feels pretty heavy for a lot of people right now. In fact, a recent survey by Talker Research revealed that Americans named “stressful” as their top word for 2026.
The survey found that one in three people (32%) are actively experiencing an existential crisis, with younger adults feeling the pressure the most. Skyrocketing living costs, global conflict, and economic uncertainty keep piling on the stress. That is precisely why lighthearted internet humor matters so much today.
Of course, what society really needs is systemic change, affordable living, and world peace. But until those things happen, memes offer a momentary breather.

Psychologists call this mental trick cognitive reappraisal. It is the act of taking a negative, overwhelming thought and looking at it from a lighter perspective.

Bitcoin bottomed near $58,000 shortly after the KOSPI peaked and has since rebounded as high as $87,000. The weekly chart shows a 20 day-period rolling correlation of -0.41, indicating a moderately negative relationship between bitcoin and the KOSPI. 
That divergence suggests that bitcoin could benefit as investors reduce exposure to the AI trade, although correlation alone does not establish capital rotation.
The latest selloff followed comments from Broadcom (AVGO) CEO Hock Tan likening speculative AI investing to gambling in a casino, according to the Financial Times. His remarks intensified concerns about whether the industry can generate returns sufficient to justify enormous infrastructure spending. In addition, advances by Chinese memory-chip rival CXMT added to fears about competition and the durability of chipmakers’ margins, according to Bloomberg. 
SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics fell around 10% and 7%, respectively. The weakness has extended into U.S. premarket trading, with memory and storage chipmakers Micron Technology (MU) and SanDisk (SNDK) both down around 3%.

90,2026-10-13,"

				(Mis)Uses of Technology			

In 2023, Mozilla released a report noting that modern U.S. cars had the worst security and privacy standards of any major technology industry the organization tracks. That was followed by a major NYT report in 2024 showing how automakers routinely hoover up oodles of consumer driving and phone info, then sell access to that data to auto insurance companies looking to justify rate hikes.
Despite a lot of noise and fury, the U.S. doesn’t do anything about this for the same reason we refuse to pass a modern privacy law or regulate data brokers: “we” (more accurately the people who purportedly democratically represent us) have repeatedly decided that making gobs of money is significantly more important that human lives, public safety, consumer privacy, or even national security.
Consumer Reports is conducting a more recent follow up study in collaboration with Northeastern University. The full study will arrive next week, but their breakdown of what they’ve found indicates the problem hasn’t gotten better — and in some cases may have gotten worse.
The researchers tested 21 vehicles and their companion mobile apps at CR’s Connecticut auto testing facility using a faraday cage to isolate and analyze vehicle communications. They found they all funneled oceans of behavioral, driving, browsing, location, and other data to a long list of industries and companies. Often without fully informing consumers:
“…almost a quarter of the vehicle apps were found to be sending out personally identifiable information, including vehicle owners’ names, vehicle identification numbers (VINs), and precise geographic locations. That information can make it easy for companies to link driving behavior to personal data profiles created by data brokers and marketers. Such profiles are routinely sold to banks, insurers, pharmaceutical companies, lenders, and retailers, who can use it for personalized loan terms and filtered bank and insurance offers, a CR and CalMatters investigation found.”
Car makers, as they’ve always done, often blame third parties for any “data leakage” and act surprised when provided details on the width and depth of the data transfer. One company, Honda, claims it put an end to the problem immediately (which in a working country, regulators would independently confirm):
“General Motors, Honda, Nissan, and Stellantis (which owns 14 auto brands, including Chrysler, Dodge, Fiat, and Jeep), said some recipients of driver data were prohibited from independently using or selling any data they are provided. But the Northeastern researchers found those restrictions aren’t necessarily effective: After they showed their findings to Honda, the company instructed one of its vendors, Amplitude, to delete all of the location data it had received and stopped sending it going forward.”
In a lot of instances (much like the “smart TV” and other markets), if consumers try to take informed action to opt out of collection and protect themselves, they lose vehicle functionality. 
Again, carmakers do this because they know the federal U.S. government won’t meaningfully hold them accountable. And the government won’t hold them accountable because the current U.S. government is too corrupt to function in the public interest — and incentivized toward apathy because it unconstitutionally exploits this dysfunction to buy data and avoid warrants. 
Despite their own products lacking privacy and security standards, these same automakers are intensely lobbying the government to ban Chinese vehicles from the U.S. market, usually under the pretense that they’re just really worried about U.S. privacy and security (Dem lawmakers in Michigan want to ban Chinese EVs from even visiting the state). 
But as we saw with the “TikTok ban,” this is all annoyingly performative. China doesn’t need TikTok, EVs, or Chinese-made drones to collect sensitive U.S. consumer data because they can already buy most of it on the open and largely unregulated market. And they can buy most of it on  the open market because the U.S. Congress is too corrupt to function in the public interest, despite ample pretense to the contrary.


	Filed Under: automakers, china, consumer reports, consumers, data brokers, EVs, privacy, security, vehicles


	Companies: consumer reports, gm, honda, nissan

My car is 20 years old. It doesn’t even know internet exists. I’m quite happy of it.

91,2026-10-13,"After years of rumors, Apple finally announced its screen-based smart home speaker — dubbed the HomeView — on October 13, 2026. It looks fairly similar to the latest Amazon Echo Show, but Apple's device is a 7-inch square touchscreen on top of a speaker base. But the HomeView is built to run a home built around Apple's HomeKit through Sxiri AI. It also looks elegantly Apple in a way the Echo Show doesn't.
The HomeView runs on an A18 processor — borrowed from the iPhone 16 — and can control lights, locks, cameras, thermostats, timers, and calls, with voice as the main way in and touch kept as a backup. Two versions are available. You can opt for the countertop model, which sits on a rounded metal speaker base and comes in space gray, silver, starlight, and rose pink. Or, there's the wall-mounted model, which drops the base entirely, though the mount is sold separately.
The HomeView ships on November 13 alongside the new Apple TV 4K, which was announced at the same time. The new HomePod mini 2 arrives slightly earlier on October 30. Apple opted to skip a filmed keynote and ran a series of launch videos on YouTube alongside a press experience in New York instead.
Remember the iMac G4 from the early 2000s? The countertop HomeView looks an awful lot like it — it's the same shape, just smaller. The device is a thin 7-inch square display held up by a single stainless steel hinge that rises out of the rounded base, which houses the speaker. It looks somewhat like an enlarged Apple Watch rather than an iPad. The bezels are narrow, the corners are rounded, and a camera sits at the top of the panel.
There is no battery inside, since the HomeView is built to be plugged in and left alone, either on a counter or fixed to a wall, which is where the second model comes in. The wall-mounted version drops the base and extra speakers and is sold in space gray and silver, while the countertop version adds starlight and rose pink. Brightness tops out at 500 nits and drops to 0.3 nits, which is the dimmest display Apple has shipped on anything. You'll appreciate it when looking at the display in the dark.
A built-in camera handles FaceTime calls and can also recognize who's approaching it to tailor the content on the screen. Walking up to the HomeView switches the display to your own calendar, messages, photos, and widgets. It can also pick you out by voice or by the iPhone in your pocket. Walk away, and the next person gets a lock screen. You'll also need to periodically unlock your iPhone or iPad to keep using it
A down-firing subwoofer and two passive radiators sit in the base alongside two full-range drivers in behind the display. BGR's reviewer praised the 2023 HomePod's audio quality but noted the 2022 HomePod mini didn't sound quite as good as competitors.
The HomeView supports a limited selection of apps (such as Calendar, Weather, and calling apps) but has no App Store. It works more like CarPlay, where you can access only some apps. The touchscreen is only there for the moments when talking is awkward. In fact, Apple analyst Mark Gurman speculates that's why Apple chose the name HomeView instead of HomePad. Apple imagines you'll use the HomeView most to run scenes you've set up, set timers, check the smart doorbell or other security cameras, send an intercom message to another room, or pull up a recipe in a reformatted Safari page while your hands are covered in flour.
Siri AI arrived in iOS 27 and replaced the rigid command-and-phrase model that made the original HomePod so irritating to live with for many. Just like on your iPhone, you can now ask Siri for something in everyday language, change your mind halfway through a sentence, and refer back to what you said earlier, and the assistant should understand the lot. It also draws from your personal context across messages, emails, contacts, and more, so it should understand you better. Most of this happens on device to keep things fast, which is thanks to the A18 chip.
Amazon has sold the Echo Show since 2017, and Google has sold smart displays since 2018. Apple is walking into a category its rivals are deeply familiar with. Plenty of people already use a Nest Hub as a kitchen screen, but Apple reckons it offers the better assistant. The HomeView works with Matter and Thread and will pair with most devices — even those with no HomeKit logo on the box. Anyone moving across from another ecosystem can keep most of their existing hardware.
Alongside the HomeView, Apple has also released a refreshed HomePod mini. The HomePod mini 2 comes in the same shape and size as the 2020 original. It is now available in green, pink, blue, white, and black. The changes are almost all about audio. Soundglow turns the light panel into a color match for whatever album is playing, or into a rain or ocean-wave pattern when a soundscape is running. A pair can serve as Mac speakers over AirPlay, or four can run as surround speakers with the new Apple TV 4K.
The old HomePod mini could not run Siri AI, but the new one will. Just not yet. It won't ship with the HomePod mini 2, so users are stuck with the old Siri which many users found to be unreliable. Apple's N1 wireless chip brings Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6 to the smart speaker, while a new ultra-wideband chip makes handing audio over from an iPhone less fiddly.
Interestingly, Apple also shined a spotlight on several smart home devices that it has co-created with LG and Schneider Electric. The Apple Home Collection offers indoor and outdoor cameras, a video doorbell, a door lock, and smart thermostats. Apple has run similar arrangements with Belkin and Logitech before.
All of these devices support Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, Thread, and Matter, and the deadbolt adds ultra-wideband so it can detect how close your iPhone is. They an always pair with your iPhone using a setup card similar to the one you see when you set up AirPods. The cameras, doorbell, and lock position Apple opposite Ring and Nest for the first time — Amazon and Google's ecosystems, respectively — at the premium end of a market, like these luxury smart home devices worth the extra money.
The HomeView is available to pre-order from today (October 13) for $299, but won't start shipping until November 13. The HomePods mini 2 is also available to pre-order from today, but will arrive slightly sooner on 30 October. The new Apple Home Collection accessories will arrive later this year at the earliest, but no exact date has been given yet. They will range in price from $199 to $349.
Announcing hardware weeks or months ahead of shipping is frustrating, but it has unfortunately become normal for Apple this year. The iPhone Duo was unveiled on September 9 and does not go on sale until October 23. So a month gap between the HomeView announcement and its delivery fits the pattern of the rest of the fall. If you are trying to fill gaps in your smart home before the cold sets in rather than waiting until November, our list of smart home devices worth installing before winter covers what is available now.
Pricing across Apple's home line has already increased in 2026. The company raised the prices for the Apple TV 4K and HomePod in June as memory chip costs climbed. Amazon and Google sell smart displays for well under $200, so at $299, the HomeView is firmly positioned at the premium end of the market."
92,2026-10-13,"Are offers like this just relying on candidates giving in to the ""sunk-cost fallacy""? We'll take a look at this issue through the lens of a frustrating account shared by a candidate about a lengthy interview process that led to a lowball offer.
Model portrayal.
This candidate went through 5 grueling rounds of interviews for a senior analyst role. This included a 4-hour technical assessment (unpaid, of course) and a presentation that had to be made to the company's 3 directors. After all of this the recruiter called to tell him that he was the unanimous first choice, but when the offer arrived, it was only for $62,000… along with unspecified promises about performance bonuses that would make up the difference. 
The candidate called the recruiter, sure there was some mistake. But the recruiter clarified that the offer was accurate and legtimate. This left the candidate feeling very put out and led to him withdrawing his offer. Frustrated, he shared his experience to this online employment discussion community, sharing his experience with readers who shared their thoughts on his specific expereince and the larger issues at play here.
See his original account below followed by some of the key responses from the original thread, then we'll talk more about the trends and issues on display here.
After five rounds of interviews for a job posted at $90k, they sent an offer for $62k and claimed the higher band was for a ""unicorn""
Story/Plasma44_Rider
He spent 4 weeks “jumping through hoops.”
I spent four weeks jumping through ridiculous hoops for a senior analyst role. I completed an uncompensated four-hour technical assessment, presented a slide deck to a panel of three directors, and had a final culture-fit chat with the regional VP. The job listing clearly stated a transparent salary bracket of eighty-five to ninety-five thousand dollars.
He was stoked about the succesful interview process, until he found the offer.
The recruiter called me yesterday morning, ecstatic to tell me I was their unanimous first choice. When the formal DocuSign package arrived in my inbox twenty minutes later the base salary was listed at sixty-two thousand, with a vague note about ""performance bonuses"" making up the differance.
But when he called the recruiter to clarify she informed him that it was accurate.
i called the recruiter immediately, thinking it was an administrative typo. She cleared her throat and told me that the top bracket was strictly reserved for an ""exceptional unicorn hire"" who exceeded every metric, and that sixty-two was competitive for my background. They knew my numbers from the initial phone screen.
The employee felt that he had lost out on more than just a job. He could directly tie a value to the process in the 15 hours of PTO he had spent on the hiring process.
They deliberately stole fifteen hours of my PTO just to lowball me by thirty grand at the finish line. I rejected the offer and withdrew. Honestly, I'm done.
The candidate reviews the offer pensively. 
Licensed media supplied as editorial context. 
I'm sure each of us can point to an interview experience of our own that left us feeling put out and disadvantaged, which was exactly how this candidate felt. I'll share my own experience like this.
During a past interview experience I had. The job was with a supplier for my current employer. After 3 rounds of interviews over 4 weeks, I was told that I would have to tell my current employer that I was applying for the role. Almost as if I had to get my current employer's “permission."" On a level this was understandable since they needed to preserve their relationship with my current employer. But why couldn't they have made that same request at the start of the process? It was almost as if they were trying to get me to back out of the process, which I found out was the case later on. They knew that my employer, their client, would not take the news well if I was made to tell them, and I think they were banking on me balking at the prospect of telling them.
See, I would learn later that they needed a reason to justify not hiring me since their preferred candidate, who already worked in an overseas branch of their organization, would need to get a visa to work locally. I had presented a significant hurdle to them, as a qualified candidate, by applying. They either needed some way to prove I wasn't qualified for a role I was clearly qualified for or they needed to get me to back out of the process.
The easiest thing was to get me to back out of the process. But that went poorly for both of us. In the end, I told my employer, which went over like a lead balloon. But even after doing that the interviewer ghosted me. I didn't hear back from them for another month, presumably while they figured out how to sort out their visa conundrum. Meanwhile, I was left up a creek without a paddle and an employer who was not-too-happy with the fact that I had been looking for a way out.
This is why lengthy interview processes like this present a considerable challenge to job seekers, even when they have gainful employment. All of that time you've invested in the process has a value before you even consider all of the PTO that an employee might need to use just to complete the process. Even if it's not about the monetary value of the time lost, just think of all that time that could have been spent with your family or friends instead, or doing something you really enjoy. 
Besides, job searches are exhausting. And when you've been out of work for a while, with the bills mounting up while you spend all of this time jumping through hoops and submitting endless job applications, you start to get a bit desperate.
So, you start to think, even when that lowball offer comes through… should I just take it? But maybe that's the entire point. 
Nothing is proven, and I still ascribe things like this to “Hanlon's Razor” a philosophical razor that tells us to ""Never attribute to malice that which is adequately explained by stupidity."" Usually, these things can primarly be explained by general incompetence of hiring managers and oranizational disfunction. After all,  but without a doubt, long interview processes that lead to nothing but disappointment have become a growing trend in the modern job market.
What's your thought on this? Do you have an experience of your own? Let me know in the comments.
This was among some of the best advice offered to the candidate.

Chinese smartphone brands faltered during Q3 2026.
Reading time: 2 minutes
SamMobile
Galaxy S26 Ultra colors (Unpacked 2026)
Samsung is currently the world's biggest smartphone brand by shipment volume, according to the latest data published by Counterpoint Research. The company captured a 23% share of the market to rank number one globally.

In the third quarter (Q3) of 2026, Samsung retained its top position with that 23% global market share. Apple ranked a close second with 22%. Xiaomi was a distant third, managing to ship only 10% of phones globally during the same period. OPPO (9%) and Vivo (8%) ranked fourth and fifth, respectively.

Counterpoint Research

Samsung ranks number one in global smartphone market in Q3 2026
                                                                                
Overall, global smartphone sales dropped by 20% during Q3 2026, which is a troubling sign for the entire industry. Counterpoint Research notes that worldwide smartphone shipments hit a 14-year low during the quarter and prices increased by 20%. This decline is largely due to the extremely high prices of memory chips and processors used in phones and other consumer electronics.

Record-breaking sales of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 series did help soften the blow for Samsung. However, analysts estimate that Samsung's smartphone division (Samsung MX) is still facing losses of around $1.8 billion in Q3 2026, even after increasing the prices of its devices.

Apple and other competitors were also forced to raise prices across their product lineups because of these escalating component costs. In the fourth and final quarter (Q4) of this year, brands are expected to focus on profits, focus on higher-end phones, make production cuts, and reduce phone launches.

95,2026-10-13,"If you thought recursive self-improvement was bad, get ready for “autonomous replication and adaptation.”"
96,2026-10-13,"Share article
Print article
The Liquor Control Board of Ontario (LCBO) generated $1.8 billion for the province in 2025-26, down $358 million from 2024-25 and $757 million from 2023-24. 
The decline follows the Ontario government’s 2023 decision to expand alcohol sales to grocery stores, convenience stores and other retailers. The LCBO also incurred an estimated $33.4 million in costs associated with holding unsold American alcohol.
The provincially owned LCBO consequently generated $1.2 billion less for taxpayers over the past two years than its 2023-2024 results might have suggested. That shortfall is five times larger than the $229 million previously forecast by Ontario’s Financial Accountability Office. Meanwhile, private retailers have added $911 million in annual sales, whereas some LCBO stores may close.
For researchers like me who study the alcohol and cannabis industries and their impacts, this situation is intriguing. But for Ontario taxpayers, the financial consequences are troubling.
Two years ago, LCBO stores dominated Ontario’s alcohol retailing, accounting for 57 per cent of a $10.3-billion provincial market. The organization also supplied 448 grocers that sold beer and wine at fixed prices.
Ontario Premier Doug Ford’s government began disrupting that arrangement in December 2023.
In a news release, Ford promised to give consumers “more choice” by allowing convenience stores, grocery stores and big-box retailers to apply for alcohol retail licenses. These retailers could sell beer, wine and ready-to-drink beverages like coolers while setting their own prices.
The expansion drew criticism from the union representing LCBO workers. In 2024, the union released a satirical video mocking the government’s alcohol retailing changes. It featured a fictional billionaire named “Callum,” an apparent reference to Loblaw chairman Galen Weston.
At the same time, the government expanded the LCBO’s wholesale responsibilities. It also paid The Beer Store $225 million to end its provincial agreement and promised $330 million to Ontario’s wineries.
Private-sector alcohol retailing subsequently expanded. Although total Ontario retail sales remain roughly $10.3 billion, around 6,700 convenience and grocery stores now sell alcohol.
The added competition has impacted the LCBO’s retail business. Sales through its own stores fell by 17 per cent in 2025-26, leaving the organization with less than half the provincial retail market. Some stores may no longer be profitable.
Meanwhile, the LCBO’s wholesale operations grew. The organization handled 10 per cent more product and paid 33 per cent higher operating costs. A changing product mix may also have affected profits as shoppers bought more beers and coolers but fewer higher-margin spirits.
Some folks would say that these changes were overdue: let businesses handle retailing and give shoppers more convenience.
Others might counter that Ford’s government has effectively spent $1.7 billion — so far — just to improve access to six-packs. They might have preferred that money go towards improving access to health care or education instead.
Regardless of where people stand on expanding alcohol sales, the LCBO’s estimated $33.4 million cost of holding devalued inventory from the United States warrants scrutiny.
Like several other provinces, Ontario halted imports of American alcohol in March 2025 in response to U.S. President Donald Trump’s tariffs. Unlike most, however, the Ford government also prohibited the LCBO from selling its existing U.S. inventory.
That left $79.1 million worth of American beverages unsold. Beyond reporting $2.9 million in write-offs for March 2025, the government did not initially disclose the full cost of holding the inventory.
In May this year, I estimated those costs might approach $20 million annually. My calculation included product write-downs, warehouse rental and implied interest payments for tied-up money.
In July, the LCBO belatedly disclosed that storage had cost $8 million so far. But it did not mention that it also wrote down products worth $17.5 million four months earlier, or that given current interest rates, simply owning these products was costing taxpayers about $2 million annually.
As of October 2026, these reports and estimates suggest that inventory write-downs totalled $20.4 million, warehousing costs were approaching $10 million and the implied interest cost was nearing $3 million.
This puts the estimated holding cost at $33.4 million over 20 months. That’s 42 per cent of the inventory’s original value. Annualized over 12 months, that works out to about $20 million, consistent with my original estimate.
Halting U.S. imports is a logical trade negotiation strategy, but banning existing product sales is hardly “prudent fiscal management.” It is more like taking 42 per cent of the bottles and emptying them on the ground.
Instead of continuing to hold the inventory, the Ontario government should allow the LCBO to sell it and recover as much as possible of the remaining $58.7 million investment. Better late than never.
The Ontario government should also stop withholding economic information that businesses need to understand their market.
The LCBO finalized its 2025-26 financial statements in June, but the Ontario government did not release them until September. By comparison, Québec publicized its liquor agency’s annual report in early June.
Ford’s government has faced repeated criticism over transparency and access to information. But this specific delay also works against the interests of the businesses he claims his policies support.
The LCBO remains Ontario’s largest alcohol wholesaler and a major retailer. Its financial statements provide valuable information to the province’s 6,700 private-sector retailers as they navigate a changing market.
Delaying those reports for three months made it harder for businesses to make informed decisions and for the public to assess the government’s policies. That undermines the government’s stated goals of creating the conditions for businesses to grow and making Ontario “open for business.”

      Professor of Operations Research, Brock University

97,2026-10-13,"Retro soda syphons and salad shooters are slowly making a comeback - with forgotten gadgets from the Sixties, Seventies and Eighties back on the market."
98,2026-10-13,"In staggering news from several sources, the famous Getty Images is applying for Chapter 11 bankruptcy and planning to go private.
As a private company, Getty will be controlled by the Getty family affiliates as well as Koch Equity Development and will also be backed by $533 million in new equity.
This comes after its proposed $3.7 billion Shutterstock merger fell apart earlier in the year. But the wider story might be that the economics of commercial imagery is being rewritten by AI.
Far from photography being dead, the background to this is that people have never consumed more photographs in history. The problem is that we've spent the last couple of decades training everyone to expect images instantly, infinitely, and for little to zero cost.
You can see this when you go back to cheap stock image companies followed by smartphones and currently free user generated images. These days it's easy enough to get an AI agent to produce you a pretty reasonable image from your phone or laptop – straight from the algorithm.
This is definitely a pattern that the wider photography community should pay attention to as images have become abundant.
And it should be separated out from good photography which still remains rarer. It puts the onus back on photographers to double and triple down on originality and approaches to photography that are not yet subsumed by AI. Being the cheapest photographer on the block is no longer a business moat.
You can read more about the situation from the horse's mouth here, but it's also being reported on here.

Updated on:  October 13, 2026 / 12:42 PM EDT
            / CBS News
          
Shares of Six Flags slid as much as 16.3% after officials confirmed that a 42-year-old man died during an incident at one of the amusement park's locations in Georgia. 
Six Flags Entertainment Corporation stock, which trades under the ticker symbol FUN, fell 12% in early trading. By late morning, the stock was trading at $9.98, down 16.3%.
The person died on Monday during a ride on one of the roller coasters at Six Flags Over Georgia, located in Austell, Georgia. According to witness testimony provided to the Cobb County Police Department, the man was ejected from the ride and struck other patrons before falling beneath the attraction. Several people were injured during the incident, according to the Cobb County Police Department.
Officials did not confirm which roller coaster was involved in the incident, although CBS News Atlanta cameras showed the park's Goliath ride, described as an ""intense 7­­­0­­­-­­­m­­­i­­­l­­­e­­­-­­­p­­­e­­­r­­­-­­­h­­­o­­­u­­­r thrill ride,"" had shut down. 
Dennis Speigel, founder and owner of consulting firm International Theme Park Services, said incidents like these are normally the result of an engineering issue, operating issue or rider responsibility issue. 
He expects it will have very little, if any, impact on sales at the theme park.
""The public knows that the company is going to go in, examine, and figure out exactly what happened on this issue,"" Speigel said, adding he's seen ""situations similar to this over the last 50 years with basically no impact.""
 An investigation is ongoing in Georgia to determine what happened Monday. 
In the short term, the company's stock is taking a hit, extending a slide that started in September. Shares are down 33.8% since the start of the year. That comes as Six Flags faces high CEO turnover in the last decade and financial struggles following a 2024 merger with Cedar Fair, Speigel said.
The ""merger really put the company in a very difficult position, [and] dug a very big hole for them,"" he said.
Following the incident in Georgia, Six Flags closed several other roller coasters across its parks, including the Goliath roller coaster in Los Angeles, CBS News LA reported.
""Out of an abundance of caution, we have temporarily closed similar attractions across our parks,"" a Six Flags spokesperson said Monday.
